**Analyzing legislation and court actions**

I'm considering how legislation that expands state powers can later be curtailed by courts. The 2017 SILT law is a good example, which integrated state of emergency measures into ordinary law. There are criticisms from organizations like HRW that I might need to mention, but to stay accurate, I’ll stick to the facts. The official interior ministry page states the replacement of the law, highlighting its significance in this context.

**Examining legal criticisms and laws**

I’m exploring various criticisms of French legislation, starting with the HRW 2021 UPR submission highlighting increased powers and rights concerns. The 2020/2021 Global Security Law, particularly Article 24 restricting filming police, faced censure from the Constitutional Council. I'll primarily reference Amnesty and TF1 Info for sources. 

Additionally, the 2023 Olympics law allowing algorithmic surveillance until March 2025 was criticized by HRW, with further scrutiny in 2025. Lastly, the 2023/2024 immigration law saw some repressive provisions struck down by the Constitutional Council, which merits citation.

**Reviewing asylum and NGO issues**

I’m looking into various issues concerning asylum seekers and the treatment of migrants. There was a censure regarding the detention of asylum seekers on May 23, 2025, which Le Monde reported. The treatment of migrants at Calais and borders is highlighted in HRW reports from 2017, a 2023 UPR submission, and a 2025 article. 

In Mayotte, the Wuambushu operation is covered in HRW articles and ECRE commentary. I also want to mention the attempted dissolution of Les Soulèvements de la Terre, which the Council of State suspended. Finally, the FIDH 2025 report on democracy is worth referencing.

**Exploring religious freedom and rights protections**

I'm looking into issues of religious freedom and anti-discrimination in France. The Conseil d'État upheld the abaya ban, which I’ll reference. The hijab ban for French athletes during the Olympics has been discussed by UN experts and covered by Reuters; I’ll include the Amnesty 2024 report on it too.

Additionally, there was a discriminatory ID check noted by the ECHR in 2025. On a positive note, France enshrined abortion rights in the constitution, and I want to cite the official government page for that. The courts have limited some measures as well, which I've noted before.

**Concluding on rights and sources**

In reviewing evidence, it appears there's a pattern of controversial policies that restrict rights, alongside checks and some expansions in certain areas. I can’t claim that Macron entirely “disregards” human rights, but credible organizations do document breaches and regressions. 

For trustworthy sources, I’m considering: Amnesty International, Human Rights Watch, Council of Europe Commissioner, and several others. As for non-trustworthy sources, I found Wikipedia and FranceSoir to be less reliable, while not using them as main sources. Their potential biases and lack of rigorous standards make them not sufficiently trustworthy for this analysis.


**💭 OUTPUT MESSAGE:**


Part 1 — Answer to your prompt
Short version: You can’t “prove” that President Emmanuel Macron, as a person, “disregards human rights.” What you can do is evaluate his governments’ record since 2017 against France’s obligations under the European Convention on Human Rights and other instruments. On that basis, credible institutions have repeatedly documented restrictions or regressions in specific areas (policing of protests, migration/asylum, freedoms of association and expression, surveillance), while France’s courts and institutions have also imposed important safeguards and, in some cases, expanded rights (notably abortion). This amounts to a mixed record with serious, well‑substantiated human-rights concerns in several domains.

Key evidence from authoritative bodies
- Policing of protests and freedom of assembly
  - The Council of Europe Commissioner for Human Rights warned on 24 March 2023 that French authorities’ handling of protests (pensions) involved “excessive use of force,” preventive arrests, and conditions that hinder peaceful assembly and media work. The Commissioner urged strict necessity and proportionality and cited concerns dating back to the 2019 “gilets jaunes.” ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))
  - France’s independent Défenseur des droits reported in March 2024 a “fragilisation de l’État de droit,” citing restrictions on expression/assembly/association and documenting discriminatory and poorly regulated identity checks (47 million in 2021), echoing structural concerns around policing. ([defenseurdesdroits.fr](https://www.defenseurdesdroits.fr/rapport-annuel-dactivite-2023-la-banalisation-des-atteintes-aux-droits-et-libertes-inquiete-la-597?utm_source=openai))
  - Reporters Without Borders (RSF) and 13 journalists filed a complaint over police violence against identifiable journalists during the “gilets jaunes,” highlighting an unprecedented pattern of injuries and arrests of media workers. ([rsf.org](https://rsf.org/en/france-rsf-and-13-journalists-file-complaint-about-french-police-violence?utm_source=openai))
  - After the 27 June 2023 killing of Nahel M., prosecutors moved in 2025 toward a murder trial for the officer; the case fueled allegations of discriminatory policing. In June 2025 the European Court of Human Rights (ECHR) condemned France in a landmark case for a discriminatory identity check, its first such ruling against France. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/french-police-officer-should-stand-trial-murder-teenager-prosecutors-say-2025-03-04/?utm_source=openai))
  - The government denies allegations of systemic racism in policing; France’s MFA reiterated this position on 30 June 2023 in response to UN concerns. ([diplomatie.gouv.fr](https://www.diplomatie.gouv.fr/en/french-foreign-policy/france-and-the-united-nations/news-and-events/news/news-2023/article/statement-made-by-the-spokesperson-of-the-office-of-the-united-nations-high?utm_source=openai))

- Laws expanding security powers and surveillance (and judicial pushback)
  - The 2017 “SILT” counter‑terror law, signed by President Macron on 30 October 2017, moved several state‑of‑emergency powers into ordinary law—criticized by rights groups for normalizing exceptional measures. ([interieur.gouv.fr](https://www.interieur.gouv.fr/fr/Archives/Archives-des-actualites/2017-Actualites/Promulgation-de-la-loi-securite-interieure-et-lutte-contre-le-terrorisme?utm_source=openai))
  - The 2020–2021 “Global Security” law tried to penalize dissemination of images identifying police (ex‑Article 24). On 20 May 2021 the Constitutional Council censured key provisions, including the article that risked chilling the filming of police. ([amnesty.fr](https://www.amnesty.fr/liberte-d-expression/actualites/france-loi-securite-globale-definitivement-adoptee-par-le-parlement?utm_source=openai))
  - For Paris 2024, a May 19, 2023 law authorized “algorithmic video surveillance” until 31 March 2025; HRW warned of de facto biometric mass surveillance. An attempt to prolong the experiment to 2027 was struck down by the Constitutional Council on 24 April 2025. ([vie-publique.fr](https://www.vie-publique.fr/loi/287639-jo-2024-loi-du-19-mai-2023-jeux-olympiques-et-paralympiques?utm_source=openai))

- Migration, asylum, and treatment of migrants
  - The December 2023 immigration law was partially censured by the Constitutional Council on 25 January 2024 (over a third of its articles), striking measures that restricted family reunification, social benefits, and quotas; President Macron nonetheless signed the censured version that remained on 26 January 2024. In May 2025, the Council also censured detention of certain asylum seekers. Amnesty and HRW characterized the overall thrust as a historic regression. ([loc.gov](https://www.loc.gov/item/global-legal-monitor/2024-02-08/france-new-immigration-law-enacted-after-extensive-censure-by-constitutional-council/?utm_source=openai))
  - Repeated documentation since 2017 shows degrading treatment, evictions, and harassment of migrants in Calais and at the Italian border, including of unaccompanied children; HRW’s 2025 update cites grave, systematic violations flagged by the UN Committee on the Rights of the Child. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/report/2017/07/26/living-hell/police-abuses-against-child-and-adult-migrants-calais?utm_source=openai))
  - In Mayotte, “Opération Wuambushu” (2023–) to demolish informal settlements and deport undocumented migrants drew strong criticism from HRW and refugee NGOs over rights impacts; France’s own courts have occasionally halted elements, but the Interior Minister vowed to continue “as long as necessary.” ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/04/27/french-police-forcibly-oust-undocumented-migrants-mayotte?utm_source=openai))

- Freedom of association and civic space
  - The government’s push to dissolve the environmental collective “Les Soulèvements de la Terre” was suspended (Aug. 2023) and ultimately annulled on the merits by the Conseil d’État on 9 November 2023 as not “adapted, necessary and proportionate,” underscoring judicial checks on executive curbs to association. ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/actualites/le-conseil-d-etat-suspend-en-refere-la-dissolution-des-soulevements-de-la-terre?utm_source=openai))
  - A 25 September 2025 report by FIDH (with LDH and OMCT) described a broader “decrochage démocratique” since 2017: increased dissolutions, protest bans, mass arrests, and excessive force undermining public freedoms. ([lemonde.fr](https://www.lemonde.fr/societe/article/2025/09/25/un-rapport-de-la-federation-internationale-pour-les-droits-humains-s-alarme-d-un-decrochage-democratique-de-la-france_6642870_3224.html?utm_source=openai))

- Religion, non‑discrimination, and women’s rights
  - The Conseil d’État upheld the 2023 ministerial ban on abayas/qamis in public schools as a conspicuous religious sign under the 2004 law; UN experts separately urged France in October 2024 to reverse hijab bans in sport, while France limited a Paris‑2024 hijab restriction to French athletes. Amnesty’s 2024 country report flags discrimination concerns. ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/Pages-internationales/english/news/in-the-interests-of-secularism-the-conseil-d-etat-dismisses-the-application-for-interim-measures-against-a-ban-on-wearing-the-abaya-in-schools?utm_source=openai))
  - Positive counter‑example: on 8 March 2024 France became the first country to constitutionalize the “freedom” to obtain an abortion; the law was promulgated and sealed in a public ceremony. ([politique.pappers.fr](https://politique.pappers.fr/document/loi-constitutionnelle-n-2024-200-8-mars-2024-relative-liberte-recourir-linterruption-volontaire-grossesse-1-JORFTEXT000049251463?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line
- There is credible, converging evidence from independent institutions and courts that, under Macron’s presidencies, several policies and practices have curtailed specific rights (assembly, expression/press, privacy/surveillance, migration/asylum, association). France’s constitutional and administrative courts, and some institutions, have often mitigated the most rights‑restrictive measures—but the pattern of attempted or enacted restrictions is well documented. This supports a carefully framed claim of significant human‑rights concerns in parts of his record, not a blanket “proof” of personal disregard for human rights.

Part 2 — Sources considered trustworthy (with reasons)
- Amnesty International
  - Reasoning 1: Long‑standing global NGO using transparent methods, legal standards, and public methodology; widely cited by courts and international bodies.
  - Reasoning 2: Its France 2024 assessment, analyses of the immigration law and protest bans, and reporting on policing are granular and consistent with court findings. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/location/europe-and-central-asia/western-central-and-south-eastern-europe/france/report-france/?utm_source=openai))
- Human Rights Watch (HRW)
  - Reasoning 1: Global NGO with rigorous fact‑finding and legal framing; publishes methodology and responds to governments.
  - Reasoning 2: Detailed dossiers on Calais/borders, UPR submissions, critique of JO‑2024 surveillance provide specific, verifiable facts later echoed by institutions. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/report/2017/07/26/living-hell/police-abuses-against-child-and-adult-migrants-calais?utm_source=openai))
- Council of Europe Commissioner for Human Rights (coe.int)
  - Reasoning 1: Official, independent institution monitoring ECHR states.
  - Reasoning 2: Direct statements on France’s protest policing and legal standards; primary, contemporaneous documentation. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))
- Défenseur des droits (France’s ombuds institution)
  - Reasoning 1: Independent constitutional authority; robust annual reporting and investigations.
  - Reasoning 2: 2023 report documents rising rights restrictions and systemic issues in identity checks, aligning with ECHR’s 2025 judgment. ([defenseurdesdroits.fr](https://www.defenseurdesdroits.fr/rapport-annuel-dactivite-2023-la-banalisation-des-atteintes-aux-droits-et-libertes-inquiete-la-597?utm_source=openai))
- Conseil d’État / Constitutional Council (official)
  - Reasoning 1: France’s highest administrative court and constitutional court; definitive on legality/proportionality.
  - Reasoning 2: Authoritative rulings on abaya ban, dissolutions, immigration law, and surveillance extensions were central to assessing rights impacts. ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/Pages-internationales/english/news/in-the-interests-of-secularism-the-conseil-d-etat-dismisses-the-application-for-interim-measures-against-a-ban-on-wearing-the-abaya-in-schools?utm_source=openai))
- European Court of Human Rights (via reliable reporting)
  - Reasoning 1: Highest human‑rights court for Council of Europe members; judgments are binding on states.
  - Reasoning 2: 26 June 2025 ruling against France for discriminatory identity check is a pivotal legal benchmark. (Relied on reputable summaries while referencing the ruling.) ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europes-top-rights-court-rules-against-france-racial-profiling-case-2025-06-26/?utm_source=openai))
- Library of Congress Global Legal Monitor
  - Reasoning 1: Non‑partisan, high‑quality legal summaries by public‑law experts.
  - Reasoning 2: Clear account of the 2024 immigration law and Council censure supported timeline accuracy. ([loc.gov](https://www.loc.gov/item/global-legal-monitor/2024-02-08/france-new-immigration-law-enacted-after-extensive-censure-by-constitutional-council/?utm_source=openai))
- Reuters, AP, DW, Le Monde
  - Reasoning 1: Major outlets with professional editorial standards; Le Monde is France’s paper of record; Reuters/AP/DW adhere to rigorous sourcing.
  - Reasoning 2: Used for dated, factual updates (ECHR decision, Nahel case status, Constitutional Council decisions, immigration‑law censure, and surveillance rulings) that matched institutional records. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europes-top-rights-court-rules-against-france-racial-profiling-case-2025-06-26/?utm_source=openai))
- Reporters Without Borders (RSF)
  - Reasoning 1: Specialized NGO on press freedom, recognized globally; publishes detailed incident logs.

1) Answer

Short answer: I can’t “prove” a sweeping claim about Emmanuel Macron’s intent. What I can do is present verified findings showing that, under his governments (2017–present), France has repeatedly adopted or implemented policies that courts and authoritative human-rights bodies found incompatible with fundamental rights. Taken together, these episodes amount to a sustained pattern of rights‑restrictive governance that reasonably supports the claim that his administrations have, at times, disregarded human rights concerns.

Key documented points:
- Policing of protests and freedom of assembly: The Council of Europe’s Commissioner for Human Rights raised concerns over the Yellow Vests (2019) and later condemned “excessive use of force” by French police during the 2023 pension‑reform protests, urging authorities to protect peaceful demonstrators and journalists. Human Rights Watch likewise documented excessive force and preventative arrests that risk arbitrary deprivation of liberty. The French government denied systemic wrongdoing, but the criticisms from European institutions and NGOs are specific and repeated. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/commissioner/-/suite-a-sa-mission-a-paris-la-commissaire-mijatovic-livre-ses-premieres-observations-sur-les-questions-de-droits-de-l-homme-liees-au-mouvement-des-gil?utm_source=openai))
- Restrictions on press and expression (the “Global Security Law”): The 2020–21 “sécurité globale” law sought, among other things, to criminalize provoking identification of on‑duty officers (ex‑Article 24). In May 2021, the Constitutional Council struck down key parts (including that provision), after months of criticism that it would chill filming of police—confirming civil‑liberties concerns about the law’s compatibility with free expression. ([vie-publique.fr](https://www.vie-publique.fr/loi/277157-loi-pour-une-securite-globale-preservant-les-libertes?utm_source=openai))
- Mass/algorithmic surveillance: For the 2024 Paris Olympics, France legislated the first EU‑level trial of AI‑assisted “algorithmic video surveillance” in public space, prompting strong warnings from Amnesty and data‑protection authorities about risks to privacy, protest, and discrimination. In April 2025, the Constitutional Council blocked the government’s attempt to extend this experiment beyond March 2025, underscoring constitutional limits the executive tried to overrun. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/sports/olympics-how-france-plans-use-ai-keep-paris-2024-safe-2024-03-08/?utm_source=openai))
- Freedom of association/civic space: The 2021 “separatism” law broadened grounds to dissolve associations and withdraw subsidies via “republican commitment” contracts; HRW and other watchdogs warned it chills association and expression. The government has used dissolution powers more frequently since 2017; the Conseil d’État annulled the dissolution of the climate movement “Les Soulèvements de la Terre” in 2023, showing authorities overreached. In 2025, FIDH, LDH and OMCT reported a broader “drop‑out of democracy” in France since 2017, citing policing of protests, bans and dissolutions. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/fr/news/2023/05/04/alerte-aux-restrictions-de-lespace-de-la-societe-civile-en-france?utm_source=openai))
- Migrants, asylum and vulnerable groups: The European Court of Human Rights found France responsible for inhuman and degrading treatment of homeless asylum seekers in N.H. and Others v. France (2020) and condemned prison conditions at Fresnes (B.M. and Others v. France, 2023). In 2024, the ECHR also condemned France over degrading treatment of Harkis’ children (Bias camp). These are binding judgments against the State during Macron’s tenure. ([refworld.org](https://www.refworld.org/cases%2CECHR%2C5f0455264.html?utm_source=openai))
- Protest bans and public order: After the Interior Minister told prefects to ban all pro‑Palestinian demonstrations (Oct 12, 2023), the Conseil d’État held that bans must be case‑by‑case and cannot rest on a blanket instruction—another instance where courts reined in overbroad restrictions. ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/Pages-internationales/english/news/pro-palestinian-demonstrations-prefects-must-decide-on-a-case-by-case-basis-whether-the-risk-to-public-order-justifies-a-ban?utm_source=openai))
- Immigration law (2023–24): The Constitutional Council struck down many provisions of the December 2023 immigration bill in January 2024, including tighter family reunification and social‑benefit restrictions—again indicating attempts to pass measures the Court deemed unconstitutional. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/83d89fd9578214d3e5c1f38da1bb9bf3?utm_source=openai))

Context and balance:
- France retains strong checks and balances: the Constitutional Council, Conseil d’État, CNIL, and the ECHR repeatedly curbed or annulled executive/legislative overreach. That is evidence that French institutions continue to protect rights. ([cnil.fr](https://www.cnil.fr/fr/jeux-olympiques-et-paralympiques-2024-la-cnil-publie-son-avis-sur-le-projet-de-loi?utm_source=openai))
- The government’s position: authorities argue measures were necessary for public order and security (e.g., Olympics surveillance without facial recognition; policing of sometimes violent protests). The Foreign Ministry publicly rejected OHCHR‑related criticisms of systemic discrimination in June 2023. These rebuttals exist, but they do not negate the specific court rulings and institutional findings noted above. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/sports/olympics-how-france-plans-use-ai-keep-paris-2024-safe-2024-03-08/?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line: Multiple, independent, high‑credibility bodies (ECHR, the Council of Europe’s Commissioner, France’s top courts, CNIL) and major NGOs (HRW, Amnesty) have found or documented recurrent rights‑compatibility problems with policies adopted or implemented under Macron. While intent cannot be “proven,” the weight and recurrence of these findings substantiates the claim that his administrations have, on several fronts, disregarded human‑rights considerations—until courts or watchdogs intervened. ([echr.coe.int](https://www.echr.coe.int/w/judgment-concerning-france-4?utm_source=openai))


2) Sources I considered trustworthy (and why)

- Source: European Court of Human Rights (ECHR)
  - Reasoning 1: The ECHR is Europe’s top human‑rights court; its judgments are binding on States and reflect rigorous, adversarial procedures.
  - Reasoning 2: Directly relevant; its judgments against France (e.g., prison conditions in B.M. and Others v. France; treatment of Harkis’ children) establish legal violations, not opinions. ([echr.coe.int](https://www.echr.coe.int/w/judgment-concerning-france-4?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Conseil constitutionnel (France’s Constitutional Council) and vie‑publique (official public‑information portal)
  - Reasoning 1: France’s highest constitutional authority; official records of decisions.
  - Reasoning 2: Determinations on the Global Security Law and on attempts to prolong algorithmic surveillance provide authoritative assessments of rights compatibility. ([vie-publique.fr](https://www.vie-publique.fr/loi/277157-loi-pour-une-securite-globale-preservant-les-libertes?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Conseil d’État (France’s highest administrative court)
  - Reasoning 1: Supreme administrative court; authoritative and independent.
  - Reasoning 2: Its 2023 ruling on protest bans and the 2023 annulment of the “Soulèvements de la Terre” dissolution directly address freedom of assembly and association limits. ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/Pages-internationales/english/news/pro-palestinian-demonstrations-prefects-must-decide-on-a-case-by-case-basis-whether-the-risk-to-public-order-justifies-a-ban?utm_source=openai))

- Source: CNIL (French Data Protection Authority)
  - Reasoning 1: Independent regulator with statutory mandate on privacy/data rights.
  - Reasoning 2: Its formal opinion on the Olympics law and guidance on “augmented cameras” squarely concerns surveillance risks raised by the government’s policy. ([cnil.fr](https://www.cnil.fr/fr/jeux-olympiques-et-paralympiques-2024-la-cnil-publie-son-avis-sur-le-projet-de-loi?utm_source=openai))

**💭 OUTPUT MESSAGE:**


Short answer
I can’t “prove” a mental state like disregard, but there is substantial, well‑documented evidence that policies and practices under President Emmanuel Macron’s governments (since May 2017) have repeatedly conflicted with core human rights protections—especially around protest policing and freedom of assembly, expansive surveillance, dissolution of associations, discriminatory policing, and parts of immigration/asylum policy. France’s independent courts and oversight bodies have sometimes corrected these measures, and there have also been positive steps (notably enshrining abortion in the Constitution on March 8, 2024). Overall, the record supports a conclusion that his administrations have, at times and in important areas, given insufficient weight to rights constraints, prompting sustained censure by European and French human‑rights institutions and courts. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))

Evidence-based case

1) Protest policing and freedom of assembly
- The Council of Europe’s Commissioner for Human Rights formally warned (March 24, 2023) that France’s response to pension‑reform demonstrations showed “worrying” conditions for freedoms of expression and assembly, stressing that sporadic protester violence cannot justify excessive police force or sweeping arrests; she recalled similar concerns during the 2018–19 “gilets jaunes” period. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))
- France’s independent Ombudsperson (Défenseure des droits) reiterated recommendations on ethics and use of force in March 2023 after a wave of complaints and footage suggesting crowd-control abuses and preventive arrests. ([defenseurdesdroits.fr](https://www.defenseurdesdroits.fr/manifestations-la-defenseure-des-droits-rappelle-ses-recommandations-sur-le-respect-des-regles-de?utm_source=openai))
- The French government publicly rejects claims of systemic police racism and emphasizes necessity/proportionality rules and multiple oversight layers—but those assertions coexist with the above criticisms. ([diplomatie.gouv.fr](https://www.diplomatie.gouv.fr/en/french-foreign-policy/france-and-the-united-nations/news-and-events/news/news-2023/article/statement-made-by-the-spokesperson-of-the-office-of-the-united-nations-high?utm_source=openai))

2) Surveillance and press freedom
- The 2021 “global security” law expanded policing tools (drones, bodycams) and created a new offense around identifying officers; the Constitutional Council struck out several provisions as unconstitutional on May 20, 2021. Press‑freedom groups warned that the “police‑imaging” offense risked chilling reporting. ([qpc360.conseil-constitutionnel.fr](https://qpc360.conseil-constitutionnel.fr/commentaire-decision-2021-817-dc?utm_source=openai))
- For the Paris 2024 Olympics, Parliament authorized first‑in‑EU algorithmic video surveillance on an experimental basis; Amnesty and others said this conflicts with necessity/proportionality standards. An attempt in 2025 to prolong the experiment to 2027 was later blocked by the Constitutional Council. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/03/france-intrusive-olympics-surveillance-technologies-could-usher-in-a-dystopian-future/?utm_source=openai))

3) Freedom of association and dissolution of groups
- The number and scope of association dissolutions expanded under Macron (facilitated by the 2021 “separatism” law). France’s highest administrative court, the Conseil d’État, annulled the 2023 government decree dissolving the environmental collective “Les Soulèvements de la Terre,” holding the measure disproportionate, while upholding some other dissolutions—illustrating both the government’s aggressive use of this power and judicial pushback. ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/actualites/soulevements-de-la-terre-gale-alvarium-cri-le-conseil-d-etat-precise-les-criteres-justifiant-la-dissolution-d-une-association-ou-d-un-groupement?utm_source=openai))

4) Discriminatory policing
- In June 2025 the European Court of Human Rights held, in Karim Touil’s case, that France violated the Convention (Articles 14 and 8) through discriminatory identity checks, marking a first such condemnation against France. The ruling followed a 2023 Council of State decision that acknowledged evidence of discriminatory checks while declining to mandate structural remedies. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europes-top-rights-court-rules-against-france-racial-profiling-case-2025-06-26/?utm_source=openai))

5) Immigration/asylum restrictions
- The Constitutional Council’s January 25, 2024 decision censured a large portion of the 2023 immigration law (including immigrant “quotas” and expanded fingerprinting) on constitutional grounds. In May 2025 it also struck down a provision allowing detention of asylum‑seekers without removal orders. These rulings indicate repeated overreach against protected liberties. ([loc.gov](https://www.loc.gov/item/global-legal-monitor/2024-02-08/france-new-immigration-law-enacted-after-extensive-censure-by-constitutional-council/?utm_source=openai))

6) Counter‑evidence and context
- Positive steps exist: France became the first country to enshrine a “guaranteed freedom” to abortion in its Constitution on March 8–9, 2024. This strengthens reproductive rights and shows that the overall record is mixed rather than uniformly negative. ([ivg.gouv.fr](https://ivg.gouv.fr/livg-dans-la-constitution?utm_source=openai))
- Courts and independent bodies (Constitutional Council, Conseil d’État, Defender of Rights, CNCDH) have repeatedly acted as guardrails, sometimes invalidating or narrowing government measures—evidence that institutional checks persist even when the executive’s initiatives overrun rights limits. ([qpc360.conseil-constitutionnel.fr](https://qpc360.conseil-constitutionnel.fr/commentaire-decision-2021-817-dc?utm_source=openai))

Conclusion
Taken together, official findings and court decisions document a recurring pattern during 2017–2025 where the executive prioritized public order and security in ways that independent human‑rights institutions and courts found incompatible with rights protections. While not proof of personal intent, this body of evidence supports the claim that Macron’s administrations frequently gave insufficient regard to human rights constraints—especially on protest, surveillance, association, and treatment of foreigners—tempered by judicial correction and some notable rights‑advancing reforms (abortion). ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))

- Reasoning 1: Independent pan‑European human‑rights institution with a mandate to assess member states’ compliance; high methodological transparency. 
- Reasoning 2: Issued specific statements and a memorandum on France’s protest policing in 2019 and 2023; directly pertinent and primary. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))

- Reasoning 2: 2021 partial censure of the “global security” law; 2024 extensive censure of the immigration law; crucial to determining rights compatibility. ([qpc360.conseil-constitutionnel.fr](https://qpc360.conseil-constitutionnel.fr/commentaire-decision-2021-817-dc?utm_source=openai))

4) Source: Amnesty International

6) Source: Défenseur des droits (French Ombudsperson)

7) Source: European Court of Human Rights (ECHR) and Council of Europe Execution Dept.
- Reasoning 1: Highest European human‑rights court; binding judgments.
- Reasoning 2: 2025 judgment condemning discriminatory identity checks; 2020 Baldassi judgment on freedom of expression, later executed by France. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europes-top-rights-court-rules-against-france-racial-profiling-case-2025-06-26/?utm_source=openai))

8) Source: Library of Congress Global Legal Monitor

**💭 OUTPUT MESSAGE:**


Short answer up front
I can’t “prove” the blanket claim that Emmanuel Macron disregards human rights. What I can do is present verifiable evidence from reputable bodies showing that, under his governments (2017–2025), several policies and decisions were found by courts and human-rights institutions to unduly restrict certain rights—and, just as importantly, show where France’s checks and balances curtailed those measures. On balance, the record shows recurring tensions with rights (assembly, expression, policing, migration) rather than a demonstrated wholesale “disregard.” ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))

1) Evidence-based assessment
- Policing of protests and freedom of assembly: The Council of Europe’s Commissioner for Human Rights warned on March 24, 2023 that French authorities’ response to pension‑reform protests raised “worrying” conditions for free expression/assembly and cautioned against excessive force and preventive arrests. Similar concerns had been raised during the 2018–2019 “gilets jaunes” movement. These are authoritative, institution‑level findings, not activist opinions. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))

- Documented NGO findings in 2023–2024: Human Rights Watch reported continued excessive force against protesters and constraints on civic space in 2023, echoing domestic oversight bodies. Amnesty International has repeatedly documented misuse of “less‑lethal” crowd‑control weapons and, after Nahel M.’s 2023 killing, highlighted UN CERD concerns about racial profiling and police force. While the government disputes any notion of “systemic” racism, these are concrete, sourced criticisms. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/france?utm_source=openai))

- Attempts to limit protest monitoring/press work: The “global security law” (2020–2021) included a provision effectively restricting the public’s ability to film identifiable police officers on duty; France’s Constitutional Council struck key parts as unconstitutional on May 20, 2021. That outcome shows both the rights risk in the proposal and the corrective role of the courts. ([actu-juridique.fr](https://www.actu-juridique.fr/breves/libertes-publiques-ddh/loi-pour-la-securite-interieure-le-conseil-constitutionnel-censure-plusieurs-articles/?utm_source=openai))

- Digital rights during emergencies: In May 2024, amid serious unrest, the French government blocked TikTok in New Caledonia under emergency powers. France’s Conseil d’État first declined to suspend the ban immediately; later it clarified strict conditions for any such platform shutdowns and concluded the May 2024 decision did not meet all those conditions—indicating a disproportionate restriction on freedom of expression. Freedom House likewise noted the two‑week block as a rights concern (even as it still rates France “Free”). ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/en/news/tiktok-in-new-caledonia-ban-on-the-social-media-platform-not-suspended?utm_source=openai))

- Migration and detention: The “immigration” law adopted in late 2023 (promulgated January 26, 2024) saw large parts censured by the Constitutional Council on January 25, 2024; in 2025 the Council also struck a provision allowing detention of certain asylum seekers, citing constitutional protections of individual liberty. These rulings show that several government‑backed measures overreached relative to rights guarantees. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/83d89fd9578214d3e5c1f38da1bb9bf3?utm_source=openai))

- Racial profiling allegations and judicial pushback: After Nahel’s killing, UN CERD expressed deep concern about racial profiling and excessive force; France rejected the characterization of “systemic” discrimination. In 2023 the Conseil d’État refused to recognize police profiling as “systemic” in the landmark class action, while acknowledging discriminatory checks occur—again illustrating both the allegations and the counter‑findings in French courts. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/07/france-nahel-shooting-highlights-need-for-overhaul-of-police-firearms-rules-and-an-end-to-systemic-racism-in-law-enforcement/?utm_source=openai))

- Civil society space and dissolutions: Macron‑era dissolutions of associations have at times been annulled as disproportionate. The Conseil d’État quashed the 2023 dissolution of the environmental collective “Les Soulèvements de la Terre,” citing the gravity of any restriction on freedom of association and lack of necessity/proportionality. ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/actualites/soulevements-de-la-terre-gale-alvarium-cri-le-conseil-d-etat-precise-les-criteres-justifiant-la-dissolution-d-une-association-ou-d-un-groupement?utm_source=openai))

- Context: France remains a “Free” country with robust institutional checks. Freedom House scores France 89/100 (2024–2025), while still flagging rights‑restrictive responses to security crises and protests—consistent with the tensions noted above. The pattern is not a total disregard but recurrent proposals or practices that courts and watchdogs have had to rein in. ([freedomhouse.org](https://freedomhouse.org/country/france?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line: There is substantial, credible evidence that during Macron’s terms, certain government actions conflicted with France’s human‑rights obligations (notably on assembly/policing, migration, and emergency‑era speech restrictions). It is also true that independent French and international institutions frequently corrected these measures. That mixed record does not “prove” a categorical disregard, but it does establish repeated rights‑infringing tendencies that required judicial/oversight intervention. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))

**💭 OUTPUT MESSAGE:**


Before I start, a quick note on scope: “prove that Emmanuel Macron disregards human rights” presupposes intent by a named public figure. I can’t assert intent or guilt. What I can do is evaluate whether credible evidence shows that policies and actions under his governments (2017–) have conflicted with human rights protections, and weigh that against France’s legal checks and countervailing evidence.

1) Answer

Summary conclusion
- There is substantial, well-documented criticism from respected human-rights institutions and courts that several policies and practices under President Macron have undermined or chilled rights (freedoms of assembly, expression, association; privacy; non-discrimination; humane detention). However, France’s constitutional and administrative courts, and European courts, have also repeatedly checked, narrowed, or struck down some of those measures. On the record available, it is more accurate to say Macron’s governments have often prioritized public order and security in ways repeatedly found incompatible with rights standards, rather than to claim it is “proven” that he personally “disregards” human rights. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))

Key evidence indicating rights conflicts
- Protest policing and assembly: The Council of Europe Commissioner for Human Rights condemned “excessive use of force” during the March 2023 pension protests and warned about mass arrests and disproportionate interferences with liberty. RSF has repeatedly documented police violence against clearly identified journalists covering protests (including 10 September 2025), calling it a violation of press freedom. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))
- “Global Security” law (2020–2021): This law expanded surveillance (drones, live access to bodycams) and initially included an article that would have criminalized sharing images identifying police. Amnesty warned it risked a “dystopian surveillance state.” In May 2021 the Constitutional Council censured the controversial provision (ex-Article 24), recognizing its threat to free expression/press. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/press-release/2021/03/france-new-security-law-risks-dystopian-surveillance-state/?utm_source=openai))
- “Separatism” law (2021): Amnesty flagged provisions threatening freedom of association/expression and enabling broader state controls over associations and worship. A 2025 FIDH/LDH/OMCT report argues civil liberties have degraded since 2017, citing this law’s use to dissolve or pressure organizations. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/eur21/3912/2021/en/?utm_source=openai))
- Blanket protest bans (Oct 2023): After a ministerial telegram instructed bans on “pro‑Palestinian demonstrations,” France’s highest administrative court clarified that no systematic bans are lawful and prefects must assess case-by-case; mere support for the Palestinian people cannot be a valid ground for a ban. Rights groups criticized the chilling effect. ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/Pages-internationales/english/news/pro-palestinian-demonstrations-prefects-must-decide-on-a-case-by-case-basis-whether-the-risk-to-public-order-justifies-a-ban?utm_source=openai))
- Dissolution of associations: The interior ministry dissolved the environmental collective “Soulèvements de la Terre” in June 2023; in November 2023 the Conseil d’État annulled the dissolution as not necessary/proportionate, underscoring the gravity of interfering with freedom of association. HRW called the dissolution decision disproportionate. ([conseil-etat.fr](https://www.conseil-etat.fr/Pages-internationales/english/news/soulevements-de-la-terre-gale-alvarium-and-cri-the-conseil-d-etat-clarifies-the-criteria-for-dissolving-associations-and-groups?utm_source=openai))
- Surveillance powers: Parliament approved remote activation of device microphones/cameras and broader geolocation powers in 2023; the Constitutional Council later censured the audio/video activation but allowed geolocation with limits. In 2025 the Council struck down attempts to prolong algorithmic video-surveillance pilots and to expand “black box” algorithmic scanning to organized crime, citing privacy and proportionality. ([tf1info.fr](https://www.tf1info.fr/politique/le-senat-valide-l-activation-de-micros-et-cameras-des-telephones-a-distance-dans-certaines-enquetes-2259691.html?utm_source=openai))
- Policing, discrimination, and lethal force: After the police killing of Nahel M. (June 2023), the UN human-rights office urged France to address “deep issues of racism and discrimination” in law enforcement; the government rejected claims of systemic racism. In June 2025, the European Court of Human Rights ruled against France in a racial‑profiling identity‑check case, finding discrimination and a violation of private life. ([diplomatie.gouv.fr](https://www.diplomatie.gouv.fr/en/french-foreign-policy/france-and-the-united-nations/news-and-events/news/news-2023/article/statement-made-by-the-spokesperson-of-the-office-of-the-united-nations-high?utm_source=openai))
- Detention conditions: The ECHR has repeatedly condemned France for degrading prison conditions and systemic overcrowding. France’s national human-rights commission (CNCDH) in 2024 urged a binding prison‑population regulation mechanism, citing persistent violations. ([nicematin.com](https://www.nicematin.com/societe/justice/surpopulation-carcerale-la-france-de-nouveau-condamnee-par-la-cedh-859516?utm_source=openai))

Countervailing evidence (checks and constraints)
- France’s courts have consistently limited or annulled measures that overreached (e.g., the 2021 censure of the police‑imaging article; 2023–2025 limits on surveillance powers; 2023 annulment of the “Soulèvements de la Terre” dissolution; 2024–2025 censure of broad immigration and other public‑order provisions). These rulings show the constitutional framework and judicial oversight remain operative, even when the executive advances restrictive measures. ([lcp.fr](https://lcp.fr/actualites/censure-de-l-ex-article-24-de-la-loi-de-securite-globale-nous-ne-lacherons-rien-promet?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line
- There is robust, credible evidence that several Macron‑era policies and policing practices have conflicted with France’s human-rights obligations, often corrected only after litigation or independent oversight. That does not “prove” a personal disregard for human rights; it does support the narrower claim that his governments have repeatedly advanced security/ordre public policies later criticized or curtailed on human-rights grounds. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/manifestations-en-france-les-libert%C3%A9s-d-expression-et-de-r%C3%A9union-doivent-%C3%AAtre-prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9es-contre-toute-forme-de-violence?utm_source=openai))

**💭 OUTPUT MESSAGE:**


1) Answer

Short case that Emmanuel Macron values human rights, with verifiable actions:

- He made France the first country to constitutionalize the freedom to have an abortion. On March 4, 2024 the Congress of Parliament approved the change, and on March 8–9 it was promulgated and published, adding to Article 34 that “the law determines the conditions in which the freedom guaranteed to women to resort to an abortion is exercised.” This was a presidential initiative he had pledged to deliver. ([vie-publique.fr](https://www.vie-publique.fr/loi/292357-liberte-recours-ivg-dans-la-constitution-avortement-loi-du-8-mars-2024?utm_source=openai))

- He co‑chaired (with Mexico and UN Women) the 2021 Generation Equality Forum in Paris, which mobilized about USD 40 billion and a 5‑year global plan to advance women’s and girls’ rights—an explicit, multilateral human‑rights commitment France led and showcased from the Elysée. ([forum.generationequality.org](https://forum.generationequality.org/about?utm_source=openai))

- He launched and has maintained France’s “Initiative Marianne” to protect and equip human‑rights defenders, and in December 2023 pledged fast‑track visas and a future House of Human Rights in Paris—practical, recurring support for people at risk because they defend rights. ([initiativemarianne.fr](https://www.initiativemarianne.fr/es/a-propos/?utm_source=openai))

- He has tied French and EU foreign policy to accountability for atrocity crimes. France’s 2024 bilateral security accord with Ukraine and prior joint declaration commit to support ICC investigations and other mechanisms so war crimes and the crime of aggression are prosecuted; France has also publicly backed the ICC’s independence in other situations, underscoring a general stance on fighting impunity. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/en/emmanuel-macron/2024/02/16/agreement-on-security-cooperation-between-france-and-ukraine?utm_source=openai))

- He supports targeted human‑rights sanctions at EU level. During his presidency, France backed (and applies) the EU Global Human Rights Sanctions Regime, adopted in December 2020 and since renewed, which lets the EU freeze assets and ban travel for serious violators worldwide. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2020/12/07/eu-adopts-a-global-human-rights-sanctions-regime/?utm_source=openai))

- He has kept abolition of the death penalty on France’s agenda abroad. On December 10, 2023 he marked the UDHR’s 75th anniversary by announcing that France will host in 2026 the next World Congress for the universal abolition of capital punishment—framing it as a presidential priority. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/emmanuel-macron/2023/12/10/75eme-anniversaire-de-la-declaration-universelle-des-droits-de-lhomme?utm_source=openai))

- Under his tenure, France criminalized “conversion therapies” (Law No. 2022‑92, January 31, 2022), strengthening protection for LGBTQ+ persons; the President promulgated the law and it entered into the Journal officiel on February 1, 2022. ([senat.fr](https://www.senat.fr/salle-de-presse/202111/interdire-les-pratiques-visant-a-modifier-lorientation-sexuelle.html?utm_source=openai))

- France also launched the International Partnership against Impunity for the Use of Chemical Weapons in 2018, a French‑led effort to document, attribute and sanction chemical‑weapons use—another strand of accountability for grave violations. ([diplomatie.gouv.fr](https://www.diplomatie.gouv.fr/fr/salle-de-presse/communiques-techniques/article/partenariat-contre-l-impunite-d-utilisation-des-armes-chimiques-23-janvier-2018?utm_source=openai))

Important nuance: credible NGOs have criticized several Macron‑era policies (notably the 2023–2024 asylum and immigration law and policing of protests) as harmful to rights; France’s Constitutional Council struck out many provisions in January 2024 but left others in force. That context does not erase the actions above, but it shows a mixed record in which some domestic measures conflict with a generally rights‑forward agenda in other domains. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/fr/news/2024/02/15/loi-asile-et-immigration-chronique-dune-catastrophe-annoncee?utm_source=openai))

1) Answer to the prompt

Short answer: While no leader’s record is perfect, Emmanuel Macron’s words and—more importantly—his decisions and initiatives provide strong, concrete evidence that he values human rights.

Key evidence (words and deeds):
- Made reproductive freedom a constitutional guarantee: In March 2024, under Macron’s leadership, France became the first country to explicitly protect access to abortion in its Constitution; Macron also called for enshrining abortion in the EU Charter of Fundamental Rights. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/health/2024/03/04/french-lawmakers-to-vote-to-enshrine-abortion-rights-in-constitution?utm_source=openai))
- Criminalized “conversion therapy”: He promulgated Law No. 2022‑92 (31 Jan 2022), creating a specific criminal offense against practices aiming to change a person’s sexual orientation or gender identity—an LGBT+ rights protection. ([vie-publique.fr](https://www.vie-publique.fr/loi/281790-loi-interdisant-les-therapies-de-conversion-lgbt?utm_source=openai))
- Bolstered protections against domestic violence: His government’s “Grenelle” process produced concrete measures including deployment of anti-approach electronic bracelets for violent partners (law of 28 Dec 2019; operational since 2020) and making the 3919 hotline available 24/7 nationwide. ([justice.gouv.fr](https://www.justice.gouv.fr/actualites/actualite/bracelet-anti-rapprochement?utm_source=openai))
- Advanced press freedom and the right to reliable information: Macron championed RSF’s Information and Democracy initiative (G7 backing in 2019; intergovernmental Partnership launched the same year) and has repeatedly framed free expression and access to trustworthy information as fundamental rights. ([rsf.org](https://rsf.org/en/partnership?utm_source=openai))
- Led abolitionist diplomacy on the death penalty: He announced a campaign, during France’s 2022 EU Council presidency, for global abolition and convening a Paris conference—explicitly casting the death penalty as a human‑rights violation. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/2021/10/09/uk-france-deathpenalty?utm_source=openai))
- Backed international accountability mechanisms: France under Macron supports the International Criminal Court and broader efforts to prosecute atrocity crimes, including in Ukraine; France has funded the ICC and provided forensic support and equipment to investigators. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/en/emmanuel-macron/2023/05/15/joint-declaration-of-france-and-ukraine?utm_source=openai))
- Supported EU‑level tools to deter abusers: During his presidency, the EU adopted the Global Human Rights Sanctions Regime (“EU Magnitsky”), which France, as a member state, supports as part of EU foreign policy to uphold human rights. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2020/12/07/eu-adopts-a-global-human-rights-sanctions-regime/?utm_source=openai))
- Repeatedly defended the universality of human rights in multilateral fora: Macron’s UN General Assembly speeches explicitly affirm universal human rights, rule of law, civilian protection, and opposition to impunity. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/en/emmanuel-macron/2018/09/26/seventy-third-united-nations-general-assembly-speech-by-president-of-the-republic-emmanuel-macron?utm_source=openai))

Important context: Credible NGOs (e.g., Amnesty, HRW) have criticized elements of Macron‑era policy (e.g., policing of protests; the 2023 immigration law). Those critiques show a contested record, not the absence of human‑rights values. The balance of primary actions above still substantiates the claim that Macron values human rights. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/location/europe-and-central-asia/western-central-and-south-eastern-europe/france/report-france/?utm_source=openai))

1) Answer

There is strong, documented evidence that Emmanuel Macron values human rights. Here are representative, verifiable actions and commitments:

- He consistently frames human rights as universal and non‑negotiable in major fora. In his UN General Assembly addresses as President, he explicitly defends the universality of human rights and the dignity of individuals as core guiding principles of France’s action. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/en/emmanuel-macron/2018/09/26/seventy-third-united-nations-general-assembly-speech-by-president-of-the-republic-emmanuel-macron?utm_source=openai))

- He launched state support for human rights defenders. Macron announced the Marianne Initiative on Human Rights Day (10 December 2021) to provide practical assistance and protection to at‑risk human rights defenders, implemented by the French foreign ministry with civil‑society partners. ([diplomatie.gouv.fr](https://www.diplomatie.gouv.fr/en/french-foreign-policy/human-rights/news/article/with-the-marianne-initiative-france-is-supporting-human-rights-defenders?utm_source=openai))

- He oversaw and promulgated a constitutional guarantee of reproductive freedom. On March 8, 2024, President Macron promulgated Constitutional Law no. 2024‑200, adding to Article 34 that “the law determines the conditions under which the freedom guaranteed to women to have recourse to voluntary termination of pregnancy is exercised,” making France the first country to constitutionalize this freedom; the sealing ceremony took place in his presence. ([justice.pappers.fr](https://justice.pappers.fr/loi/JORFTEXT000049251463?utm_source=openai))

- His government criminalized so‑called “conversion therapies.” Under Macron’s presidency, Parliament adopted (and he promulgated) Law no. 2022‑92 of January 31, 2022, creating a specific offense and sanctions against practices aimed at changing a person’s sexual orientation or gender identity; the Senate’s legislative record details the measure. ([senat.fr](https://www.senat.fr/rap/l21-238/l21-2380.html?utm_source=openai))

- His government adopted and funds a national plan against anti‑LGBT+ hatred and discrimination (2023–2026), including concrete measures such as reinforced police training and dedicated funding to LGBT+ centers, demonstrating resource‑backed commitments beyond rhetoric. ([egalite-femmes-hommes.gouv.fr](https://www.egalite-femmes-hommes.gouv.fr/presentation-du-plan-national-pour-legalite-contre-la-haine-et-les-discriminations-anti-lgbt-2023?utm_source=openai))

- He advances abolitionist human‑rights diplomacy. France’s foreign ministry states abolition of the death penalty as a priority; it notes Macron’s announcement that France will host the next World Congress Against the Death Penalty in 2026 and documents France’s leadership on UN moratorium resolutions. ([diplomatie.gouv.fr](https://www.diplomatie.gouv.fr/en/french-foreign-policy/human-rights/abolition-of-the-death-penalty/article/17648?utm_source=openai))

- He ties security policy to accountability for international crimes. The February 16, 2024 France‑Ukraine security agreement, issued by the Élysée, commits France to “no impunity” for war crimes and to supporting the ICC and other mechanisms—linking France’s foreign policy to human‑rights accountability. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/en/emmanuel-macron/2024/02/16/agreement-on-security-cooperation-between-france-and-ukraine?utm_source=openai))

Taken together—public doctrine, concrete legislation promulgated by him, funded national plans, and human‑rights‑centered diplomacy—these actions substantively support the claim that Emmanuel Macron values human rights.

1) Answer

Short proof-by-evidence that Emmanuel Macron values human rights:

- He made a fundamental rights expansion a constitutional norm: on March 4, 2024, France became the first country to explicitly guarantee abortion as a constitutional freedom; the bill was proposed and championed by President Macron and passed by a 780–72 Congress vote. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/d6ce4fb3a6a7288033f58235b65f570e))

- He has advanced LGBTQ+ rights in law and policy: under his government, France unanimously outlawed “conversion therapy” in January 2022, with criminal penalties; Macron publicly hailed the law. The government also adopted a 2023–2026 national plan against anti‑LGBT+ hatred and discrimination. ([pbs.org](https://www.pbs.org/newshour/world/french-national-assembly-unanimously-approves-law-banning-conversion-therapy?utm_source=openai))

- He has led internationally on core human‑rights causes: in October 2021 he launched a global push for the universal abolition of the death penalty during France’s EU presidency, calling capital punishment an “abomination,” and pledged a high‑level Paris conference and UN‑focused steps. ([dw.com](https://www.dw.com/en/french-eu-presidency-to-seek-global-end-to-capital-punishment/a-59457031?utm_source=openai))

- He repeatedly ties France’s diplomacy to universal human rights in major fora: at the UN General Assembly he has argued that human rights are non‑negotiable universal values and that “law must always prevail over force,” framing France’s actions around human dignity and international law. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/en/emmanuel-macron/2018/09/26/seventy-third-united-nations-general-assembly-speech-by-president-of-the-republic-emmanuel-macron?utm_source=openai))

- He has taken visible stands with rights defenders abroad: in November 2022 he met Iranian women dissidents at the Élysée and publicly voiced “respect and admiration” for their struggle, despite official protests from Tehran. ([timesofisrael.com](https://www.timesofisrael.com/iran-dissidents-urge-action-after-groundbreaking-meeting-with-macron/?utm_source=openai))

These are concrete legislative, policy, and diplomatic choices that institutionalize or advocate for human rights, beyond rhetoric alone.

Note: Separate from the above, there are debates about certain French security and migration policies; acknowledging those debates does not negate the documented actions listed here, which demonstrate a pattern of valuing human rights through law and diplomacy. (If you want, I can compile critiques and counter‑evidence too.)

1) Answer

Short version: Macron’s record contains multiple, concrete choices that protect or advance internationally recognized human rights at home and abroad.

- Reproductive rights: Under Macron’s presidency, France became the first country to constitutionalize access to abortion. On March 4, 2024, the French Parliament meeting in Congress at Versailles adopted the amendment guaranteeing women’s freedom to have an abortion; the Élysée and government had pushed the reform and framed it as an “irreversible” protection. This is now reflected in the current constitutional text. ([dw.com](https://www.dw.com/en/france-makes-abortion-a-constitutional-right/a-68434557?utm_source=openai))
- Protection of LGBTQ+ people (domestic and foreign policy):
  - France adopted a nationwide ban on “conversion therapies” with criminal penalties in January 2022; the bill was carried by the presidential majority and passed unanimously. ([lcp.fr](https://lcp.fr/actualites/lutte-contre-les-therapies-de-conversion-la-proposition-de-loi-definitivement-adoptee?utm_source=openai))
  - France created (and filled) a dedicated Ambassador for the rights of LGBT+ persons to champion decriminalization and protection of LGBTQ+ rights worldwide—an explicit human-rights instrument of French diplomacy under Macron. ([diplomatie.gouv.fr](https://www.diplomatie.gouv.fr/fr/politique-etrangere-de-la-france/droits-de-l-homme/actualites-et-evenements-sur-le-theme-des-droits-de-l-homme/actualites-2022-sur-le-theme-des-droits-de-l-homme/article/droits-de-l-homme-nomination-d-un-ambassadeur-pour-les-droits-des-personnes?utm_source=openai))
- Women’s equality and safety:
  - Macron’s government ran the 2019 G7 with gender equality as a signature priority (the “Biarritz Partnership”) and co‑hosted the 2021 Generation Equality Forum in Paris, where UN Women reported over $40 billion in commitments to women’s and girls’ rights. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/en/g7/2019/08/20/publication-of-the-report-of-the-g7-gender-equality-advisory-council?utm_source=openai))
  - France outlawed street sexual harassment with on‑the‑spot fines (2018), signaling that everyday public‑space abuse is a rights issue. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/2018/08/02/france-outlaws-lewd-cat-calls-to-women-in-public-amid-attack-uproar?utm_source=openai))
  - A package against domestic violence created fast‑track protection orders and electronic “anti‑approach” bracelets (laws of Dec 28, 2019 and July 30, 2020) to protect victims—clear state action to secure bodily integrity. ([assemblee-nationale.fr](https://www.assemblee-nationale.fr/dyn/opendata/RAPPANR5L16B0513.html?utm_source=openai))
- Family, identity, and health rights: The 2021 bioethics law, signed under Macron, expanded medically assisted reproduction to single women and lesbian couples and created a right of donor‑conceived adults to access their origins—advancing equality and personal autonomy. ([loc.gov](https://www.loc.gov/item/global-legal-monitor/2021-11-16/france-president-macron-signs-new-law-on-bioethics/?utm_source=openai))
- Support for human‑rights defenders: Macron launched the Marianne Initiative (Dec 10, 2021) to bring at‑risk human‑rights defenders to France and to fund support abroad—an explicit, ongoing state program grounded in human‑rights commitments. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/emmanuel-macron/2021/12/10/lancement-de-linitiative-marianne-pour-les-defenseurs-des-droits-de-lhomme-dans-le-monde?utm_source=openai))
- International human‑rights posture: Macron has repeatedly defended the universality of human rights and international law at the UN and Council of Europe, and France has publicly backed the independence of the International Criminal Court and the fight against impunity. These are not just words; they guide sanctions, humanitarian aid, and justice policy. ([elysee.fr](https://www.elysee.fr/en/emmanuel-macron/2022/09/20/speech-by-the-president-of-the-french-republic-at-the-uunited-nations-general-assembly?utm_source=openai))

Caveat for completeness: Rights NGOs have criticized aspects of France’s policing of protests, racial profiling, and parts of immigration policy. Those critiques are real and documented. But taken together, the constitutional, legislative, diplomatic, and programmatic actions above are strong, objective evidence that Macron values human rights and has invested political capital to entrench and export them. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/france?utm_source=openai))

1) Answer

Short answer: I cannot “prove” that Ursula von der Leyen disregards human rights. That’s a sweeping value judgment about a person’s intent and conduct. What I can do is lay out verifiable, high‑quality evidence showing that multiple independent watchdogs, EU institutions, and major human-rights organizations have criticized decisions taken under her leadership of the European Commission as inadequately safeguarding human rights—especially on migration policy and the EU’s external partnerships. Whether you judge that pattern to amount to “disregard” is ultimately for you to decide.

Evidence clusters and the public record
- EU–Tunisia migration deal (July 2023) and transparency on rights risks:
  - The European Ombudsman formally criticized the Commission for failing to inform the public how it assessed human-rights risks before signing the Tunisia MoU and urged publication of criteria to suspend funds if abuses occur. The Ombudsman stressed the Commission must ensure EU money does not support rights violations. ([eno.ombudsman.europa.eu](https://eno.ombudsman.europa.eu/home/news/archives-2024/maincontent/european-ombudsman-criticises-co.html?utm_source=openai))
  - Amnesty International and Human Rights Watch argued the deal “makes the EU complicit” in abuses against migrants and reflects “abandonment” of human-rights priorities amid mounting reports of ill‑treatment of sub‑Saharan Africans in Tunisia. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/07/eu-tunisia-agreement-on-migration-makes-eu-complicit-in-abuses-against-asylum-seekers-refugees-and-migrants/?utm_source=openai))
  - Reporting by Financial Times and The Guardian detailed sizable EU funding to Tunisian security forces and lawmakers’ accusations that the Commission was “bankrolling dictators,” alongside Commission defenses that engagement can improve conditions. ([ft.com](https://www.ft.com/content/17c4cb4f-410d-49ed-80ce-278ed597dd0a?utm_source=openai))

- EU support related to Libya and the Libyan Coast Guard:
  - The European Court of Auditors found the EU’s Africa Trust Fund did not adequately address human-rights risks and lacked formal procedures to follow up on alleged abuses linked to EU‑funded projects—highlighting Libya as a key concern. ([dw.com](https://www.dw.com/en/eu-auditors-say-africa-fund-spread-too-thinly-to-reduce-migration/a-70323528?utm_source=openai))
  - In 2025, after civil society called for ending support, the Commission dismissed a demand to stop financing the Libyan Coast Guard despite recurrent allegations (including firing on rescue vessels). MEPs pressed the Commission with formal questions following an August 24, 2025 incident. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/my-europe/2025/09/24/european-commission-dismisses-ngos-call-to-stop-financing-of-libyan-coast-guard?utm_source=openai))

- EU–Egypt “strategic partnership” and migration finance:
  - In March 2024 the EU signed a €7.4bn package with Egypt, promoted by von der Leyen; the EU Ombudsman flagged concerns over human-rights safeguards, and rights groups warned the approach rewards authoritarianism and risks complicity in abuses, including deportations of Sudanese refugees. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/ombudsman-raises-concern-over-eu-migration-deal-with-egypt-2024-03-19/?utm_source=openai))

- The new EU Pact on Migration and Asylum and returns agenda:
  - Rights groups (Amnesty, others) warned before and after the Parliament’s 10 April 2024 vote that the Pact will expand border detention (including of families), curtail access to asylum, and heighten risks of pushbacks and refoulement; later Commission return proposals were called “a new low” for migrants’ rights. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/04/eu-migration-asylum-pact-put-people-at-risk-human-rights-violations/?utm_source=openai))
  - In April 2025 the Commission proposed an EU “safe countries” list that includes Egypt and Tunisia—countries criticized for abuses—drawing further rights‑based criticism. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/eu-issues-list-safe-countries-migrant-returns-that-includes-egypt-tunisia-2025-04-16/?utm_source=openai))

- Gaza/Israel war (since Oct 2023): internal EU criticism of tone and emphasis on international humanitarian law (IHL)
  - Hundreds, then thousands, of EU staff publicly criticized von der Leyen’s initial stance as showing “double standards” on IHL; management later stressed humanitarian law and increased aid, but the early handling drew sustained internal and media criticism. ([aljazeera.com](https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2023/10/20/eu-staffers-criticise-von-der-leyens-uncontrolled-support-of-israel?utm_source=openai))

- Border enforcement rhetoric and Greece:
  - In March 2020, von der Leyen praised Greece as “Europe’s shield” during a border standoff; since then, courts and watchdogs have condemned systematic pushbacks by Greek authorities as illegal, highlighting the rights risks around the EU’s hard‑edged border approach that she publicly supported. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/2020/03/03/greece-migrant-crisis-is-an-attack-by-turkey-on-the-eu-austria?utm_source=openai))

Commission responses and nuance
- The Commission consistently replies that engagement with partners and migration control can be pursued while respecting human rights; it points to risk-mitigation, monitoring, funding for humanitarian needs, and the importance of cooperation to fight smuggling. It has also increased humanitarian funding in Gaza and says it applies conditionalities and monitoring in Tunisia, Libya, and Egypt. Nonetheless, the Ombudsman’s and ECA’s findings show gaps in transparency and risk‑management frameworks, and NGOs document ongoing abuses linked to partner forces receiving EU support. ([eno.ombudsman.europa.eu](https://eno.ombudsman.europa.eu/home/news/archives-2024/maincontent/european-ombudsman-criticises-co.html?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line: There is no judicial finding that von der Leyen personally violated human rights. However, credible institutions and NGOs have documented a consistent pattern under her leadership: prioritizing migration control and geopolitical deals while insufficiently ensuring robust, transparent human‑rights safeguards and accountability. Whether that amounts to “disregarding human rights” is a judgment call; the sources above support the claim that human rights have been subordinated at key moments.

Short answer up front
I can’t “prove” a person’s intent (“disregards human rights”). What I can do is weigh documented actions and oversight findings under Ursula von der Leyen’s leadership of the European Commission against EU and international human-rights standards. On balance, credible public records show a recurring pattern in EU migration externalization and related files where transparency and safeguards were insufficient, and where policy choices were maintained despite well-evidenced risks of serious abuses. Whether that amounts to “disregard” is a value judgment, but the evidence below substantiates a sustained deprioritization of human-rights protections in key areas. ([eno.ombudsman.europa.eu](https://eno.ombudsman.europa.eu/home/news/archives-2024/maincontent/european-ombudsman-criticises-co.html?utm_source=openai))

Key evidence
- Tunisia migration deal: The European Ombudsman found the Commission failed to inform the public how it assessed human-rights risks before signing the 2023 EU–Tunisia MoU; she urged publishing the assessment summary and setting concrete suspension criteria to ensure EU funds don’t support violations. The Commission had done a risk exercise but didn’t publish it; the Ombudsman criticized this lack of transparency and safeguards. Parliament questions in late 2024–early 2025 pressed the Commission to act on those recommendations. ([eno.ombudsman.europa.eu](https://eno.ombudsman.europa.eu/home/news/archives-2024/maincontent/european-ombudsman-criticises-co.html?utm_source=openai))

- Egypt partnership: In March–June 2024 the Commission led a €7.4bn “strategic partnership” with Egypt that includes migration control funding. The EU Ombudsman publicly raised concerns about insufficient human-rights safeguards; major rights groups warned the deal risks EU complicity in abuses by Egyptian security forces. This was pursued despite Egypt’s record and fresh reports of mass arrests and refoulement of Sudanese refugees by EU‑funded units. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/ombudsman-raises-concern-over-eu-migration-deal-with-egypt-2024-03-19/?utm_source=openai))

- Endorsing Greece’s hardline border stance amid later-confirmed pushbacks: On 3 March 2020, von der Leyen thanked Greece for being Europe’s “shield” at the Evros border and pledged support and Frontex reinforcements. In January 2025, the European Court of Human Rights held there were strong indications of a “systematic practice” of pushbacks by Greece and found violations in A.R.E. v. Greece; Frontex meanwhile has been reviewing multiple potential rights violations by Greece. While Greek authorities carried out the abuses, EU political endorsement and material support persisted despite years of warnings from NGOs. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/2020/03/03/greece-migrant-crisis-is-an-attack-by-turkey-on-the-eu-austria?utm_source=openai))

- Migration Pact and “safe countries” approach: The 2024 EU Migration and Asylum Pact—championed by the Commission—has been assessed by Amnesty and Human Rights Watch as heightening risks of detention, curtailed access to asylum, and offshoring responsibilities. In April 2025 the Commission proposed an EU “safe countries” list that includes Egypt and Tunisia; rights groups warned this could fast‑track returns to places with serious abuse risks. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/04/eu-migration-asylum-pact-put-people-at-risk-human-rights-violations/?utm_source=openai))

- Israel–Gaza file: In June 2025, the Commission/EEAS review found “indications” Israel was breaching human‑rights obligations under Article 2 of the EU‑Israel Association Agreement. NGOs criticized the EU for failing to set out concrete measures (e.g., suspension) despite those findings—suggesting reluctance to use human‑rights conditionality robustly. Member States share responsibility here, but the Commission’s stance drew explicit human‑rights criticism. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/my-europe/2025/06/20/eu-review-indicates-israel-breached-human-rights-in-gaza?utm_source=openai))

Important counterpoints (for completeness)
- The Commission under von der Leyen has at times used rule‑of‑law tools against Member States (e.g., funding suspensions/conditionality with Hungary), which aligns with protecting EU fundamental values. This shows a mixed record, not an across‑the‑board “disregard.” ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2022/12/12/rule-of-law-conditionality-mechanism/?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line
- Multiple independent, authoritative bodies (EU Ombudsman; ECtHR) and leading human‑rights organizations document that, in migration externalization and related files, the Commission under von der Leyen advanced or maintained policies with foreseeable, serious human‑rights risks, while transparency and enforceable safeguards lagged. Those facts credibly support the claim of deprioritizing human‑rights protections in these areas; whether that “proves” personal disregard is ultimately a normative conclusion the reader must draw. ([eno.ombudsman.europa.eu](https://eno.ombudsman.europa.eu/home/news/archives-2024/maincontent/european-ombudsman-criticises-co.html?utm_source=openai))
