**💭 OUTPUT MESSAGE:**


1) Answer

I cannot honestly “prove” that Ursula von der Leyen personally disregards human rights. There is no court ruling or formal finding establishing that claim. What can be shown, however, is that under her leadership the European Commission advanced policies that credible watchdogs say insufficiently safeguarded human rights—or risked facilitating abuses—especially in migration control and external partnerships. Key examples:

- EU–Tunisia migration deal (signed 16 July 2023, championed by von der Leyen). The European Ombudsman later criticized the Commission for failing to inform the public how it assessed human-rights risks and urged concrete suspension criteria for funding when abuses occur. Rights groups argue the deal risks making the EU complicit amid well-documented abuses against Black African migrants in Tunisia. The Commission’s own pages confirm the funding and scope of the MoU. These facts show, at minimum, weak human-rights due diligence and transparency around a signature initiative she promoted. ([eno.ombudsman.europa.eu](https://eno.ombudsman.europa.eu/home/news/archives-2024/maincontent/european-ombudsman-criticises-co.html?utm_source=openai))

- EU–Egypt “Strategic and Comprehensive Partnership” (announced March 2024). The EU Ombudsman publicly raised concerns about inadequate human-rights safeguards; Amnesty and other monitors documented mass arrests and deportations of Sudanese refugees by Egyptian forces benefiting from EU support, warning of EU complicity if safeguards are not enforced. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/ombudsman-raises-concern-over-eu-migration-deal-with-egypt-2024-03-19/?utm_source=openai))

- New Pact on Migration and Asylum (final European Parliament vote on 10 April 2024). Amnesty and a broad NGO coalition warned the reforms would heighten risks of pushbacks, arbitrary detention, and erosion of access to asylum across the EU. While the Pact is an EU co‑legislated package (not a personal act), the Commission initiated and steered it under von der Leyen. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/04/eu-migration-asylum-pact-put-people-at-risk-human-rights-violations/?utm_source=openai))

- Oversight of EU migration funding. The European Court of Auditors’ Special Report 17/2024 found the EU Trust Fund for Africa did not properly address human‑rights risks and lacked systems to record and follow up alleged violations—issues highly relevant to North Africa migration programming advanced during her term. ([eca.europa.eu](https://www.eca.europa.eu/ECAHTMLNews/NEWS-SR-2024-17/en/body.html?utm_source=openai))

- Context: persistent allegations of unlawful “pushbacks” by EU’s border agency Frontex (exposed by OLAF leaks and media) illustrate systemic risk of abuses in EU border control that the Commission, as guardian of the Treaties, is expected to check. This does not show personal intent, but it underlines why robust, transparent safeguards in Commission‑led migration policy are critical. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/my-europe/2022/07/29/leaked-report-finds-frontex-covered-up-illegal-migrant-pushbacks-by-greek-authorities?utm_source=openai))

Counter‑evidence and Commission position: The Commission stresses that partnerships aim to promote stability while respecting international and humanitarian law, and it has announced humanitarian measures (e.g., Gaza aid increases) and said Israel’s right of self‑defense must be exercised “in full respect of international humanitarian law.” These official positions complicate any claim that von der Leyen “disregards” human rights as a matter of intent. ([enlargement.ec.europa.eu](https://enlargement.ec.europa.eu/news/european-commission-will-immediately-triple-humanitarian-assistance-gaza-over-75-million-euros-2023-10-16_en?prefLang=nl&utm_source=openai))

Bottom line: There is substantial, well‑documented criticism from independent institutions and NGOs that policies prioritized under von der Leyen have not adequately safeguarded human rights, especially in migration externalization. That is strong evidence of policy choices with adverse human‑rights implications—but it does not amount to proof that she personally “disregards human rights.” If you want, I can compile only adjudicated findings (court or ombudsman decisions) or focus on a single file (e.g., the Tunisia MoU) to sharpen the case.

Short answer
I cannot prove the blanket claim that Ursula von der Leyen “disregards human rights.” What the public record shows is a contested picture: under her leadership, the European Commission advanced measures that human-rights groups and the EU Ombudsman say risk or insufficiently safeguard human rights (especially on migration deals and the EU asylum reforms), while at the same time her Commission also pushed and delivered human‑rights–advancing legislation and rule‑of‑law enforcement inside the EU. The evidence therefore supports a nuanced assessment rather than a categorical “proof.”

What the evidence shows (with sources)
- EU migration pacts and third‑country deals drew serious human-rights warnings.
  - Amnesty International warned ahead of the April 10, 2024 Parliament vote that the EU Pact on Migration and Asylum would expand border detention, weaken protections and heighten the risk of violations throughout the asylum chain. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/04/eu-migration-asylum-pact-put-people-at-risk-human-rights-violations/?utm_source=openai))
  - The European Ombudsman found the Commission failed to inform the public how it assessed human-rights risks before the 2023 EU‑Tunisia migration MoU, and urged clear criteria to suspend EU funding if abuses occur. ([eno.ombudsman.europa.eu](https://eno.ombudsman.europa.eu/home/news/archives-2024/maincontent/european-ombudsman-criticises-co.html?utm_source=openai))
  - Reporting the same decision, Euronews noted the Ombudsman’s call for “concrete criteria” to suspend funds and highlighted continuing abuse reports in Tunisia. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/my-europe/2024/10/23/eu-watchdog-issues-criticism-of-eu-tunisia-deal-over-human-rights-concerns?utm_source=openai))
  - The Commission signed an €8 billion partnership with Egypt in March 2024; rights groups warned that without robust safeguards the EU risks complicity in Egypt’s abuses against refugees and migrants. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/e4b3daca0df53a0ec537d92cd083bc01?utm_source=openai))
  - The EU Ombudsman publicly raised concerns about the EU‑Egypt deal’s human-rights safeguards as well. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/ombudsman-raises-concern-over-eu-migration-deal-with-egypt-2024-03-19/?utm_source=openai))
  - Investigations by NGOs and media have documented how EU funding and cooperation with North African authorities coincide with pushbacks, mass arrests and desert abandonments of migrants; HRW argued the Tunisia deal “pays lip service to human rights.” ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/09/28/eu-commission-should-stop-ignoring-tunisias-abuses-against-migrants?utm_source=openai))

- Conditions at EU borders and enforcement raise systemic rights concerns.
  - The EU Agency for Fundamental Rights (FRA) reported in July 2024, and again in its 2025 update, that serious, recurrent and widespread abuses at EU borders often are not properly investigated, and outlined steps needed to ensure effective inquiries. ([fra.europa.eu](https://fra.europa.eu/it/news/2024/investigations-rights-violations-eu-borders-remain-ineffective?utm_source=openai))

- Gaza/Israel: initial messaging drew criticism; later statements included humanitarian law language.
  - Politico reported EU capitals’ anger after von der Leyen’s unscheduled October 2023 Israel trip for not echoing calls to respect international law; hundreds of EU staff later signed a letter criticizing the Commission’s handling of the Gaza crisis. ([politico.eu](https://www.politico.eu/article/eu-governments-fume-at-queen-ursula-von-der-leyen/?utm_source=openai))
  - Days later, she publicly set out “five principles for Gaza,” including “no sustained blockade” and “no forced displacement,” aligning with core humanitarian-law concerns. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/my-europe/2023/11/06/no-hamas-rule-no-blockade-von-der-leyen-proposes-five-principles-for-gazas-future?utm_source=openai))

- Countervailing evidence: the same Commission advanced human-rights–relevant laws and rule‑of‑law enforcement.
  - The EU formally adopted the Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive in 2024, requiring large companies to identify, prevent and remedy adverse human‑rights and environmental impacts in their value chains (with penalties for non‑compliance). ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/24/corporate-sustainability-due-diligence-council-gives-its-final-approval/?utm_source=openai))
  - On rule of law inside the EU, funds for Hungary remained frozen or were even lost under the conditionality mechanism due to unresolved concerns; some funds were unblocked only after judicial reforms, with large sums still withheld. ([dw.com](https://www.dw.com/en/eu-denies-hungary-a-billion-euros-after-reform-failures/a-71196557?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line
- The documented record substantiates strong, credible criticism that the migration pacts and some third‑country deals prioritized deterrence and cooperation with abusive partners over robust, transparent human‑rights safeguards. That is serious and deserves scrutiny and reform. ([eno.ombudsman.europa.eu](https://eno.ombudsman.europa.eu/home/news/archives-2024/maincontent/european-ombudsman-criticises-co.html?utm_source=openai))
- At the same time, there is also clear, contemporaneous evidence of the Commission advancing human‑rights–protective legislation and enforcing EU values domestically. On that fuller record, I cannot “prove” a generalized disregard for human rights by von der Leyen; the fair conclusion is that her record is mixed and contested. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/24/corporate-sustainability-due-diligence-council-gives-its-final-approval/?utm_source=openai))

Short answer up front
I can’t “prove” that Ursula von der Leyen disregards human rights. What I can do is present the best-documented criticisms and the countervailing record, with sources you can check.

What the public record shows
- Externalizing EU migration control despite serious rights risks
  - As Commission President, von der Leyen fronted the 16 July 2023 EU–Tunisia Memorandum of Understanding and subsequent funding to Tunisian authorities for border control and returns. The Commission frames this cooperation as being “in full respect of international law,” and lists equipment, training and UNHCR/IOM components. ([enlargement.ec.europa.eu](https://enlargement.ec.europa.eu/news/european-union-and-tunisia-political-agreement-comprehensive-partnership-package-2023-07-16_en?utm_source=openai))
  - The European Ombudsman criticized the Commission for failing to be transparent about how it assessed human-rights risks before the Tunisia deal, and urged publication of criteria for suspending funds if abuses occur. ([eno.ombudsman.europa.eu](https://eno.ombudsman.europa.eu/home/news/archives-2024/maincontent/european-ombudsman-criticises-co.html?utm_source=openai))
  - Human rights organizations have documented repeated collective expulsions and other abuses against migrants in Tunisia and argue that EU-funded cooperation, championed by von der Leyen, lacks sufficient safeguards and makes the EU complicit. These groups call Tunisia “not a place of safety” for disembarkation. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/tunisia?utm_source=openai))
  - This evidence shows a policy choice that prioritizes migration control while accepting significant, known rights risks. It does not, by itself, legally prove that von der Leyen personally “disregards” human rights, especially given the Commission’s repeated claim of compliance with international law and funding for protection channels. ([enlargement.ec.europa.eu](https://enlargement.ec.europa.eu/news/commission-announces-almost-eu127-million-support-implementation-memorandum-understanding-tunisia-2023-09-22_en?utm_source=openai))

- The EU Pact on Migration and Asylum (2024)
  - The Pact was approved by the European Parliament in April 2024; the Commission hails it as an “orderly and humane” framework. Rights groups warn the border procedures and emergency derogations heighten risks of detention, pushbacks and refoulement, and further externalize protection obligations. Both things can be true: it is a tighter management system that, as designed, increases rights risks unless tightly implemented and monitored. ([lemonde.fr](https://www.lemonde.fr/en/international/article/2024/04/11/european-parliament-approves-migration-and-asylum-pact_6668051_4.html?utm_source=openai))

- Greece border “pushbacks” and EU messaging
  - In March 2020, amid the Evros crisis, von der Leyen thanked Greece for being Europe’s “shield,” while rights groups warned about unlawful pushbacks and suspension of asylum access. In 2025 the European Court of Human Rights found Greece had engaged in “systematic” pushbacks in the Evros region and condemned Greece in a landmark case. The Commission President’s rhetoric and emergency support in 2020 did not cause those violations, but critics argue it signaled tolerance for rights-infringing practices at EU borders. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/2020/03/03/greece-migrant-crisis-is-an-attack-by-turkey-on-the-eu-austria?utm_source=openai))

- Gaza: initially one‑sided optics, later human‑rights framing and aid
  - Von der Leyen was criticized by hundreds of EU staff in October 2023 for appearing to back Israel without simultaneously and clearly emphasizing international humanitarian law; later statements and actions explicitly invoked IHL, increased EU humanitarian aid to Gaza, and (in 2024–2025) supported a ceasefire and explored trade measures. The record is mixed: initial optics drew internal and public rights‑based criticism, followed by a more explicit human‑rights framing and policy moves. ([irishtimes.com](https://www.irishtimes.com/world/middle-east/2023/10/20/eu-staff-members-express-fury-over-von-der-leyen-stance-on-israel-hamas-conflict/?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line
- There is substantial, credible evidence that under von der Leyen’s leadership the EU has pursued migration control partnerships (notably with Tunisia) and a new Pact that, as designed or implemented, create elevated risks of human‑rights violations. There is also evidence that she and the Commission have, in other contexts, asserted international humanitarian law constraints and funded protection/humanitarian aid. On balance, the record supports strong criticism of policy choices and safeguards—not a definitive “proof” that she personally disregards human rights.

If you want, I can turn this into a pro/con brief focused on one dossier (Tunisia MoU, the Pact, or Gaza) and map specific decision points against EU Charter obligations.

Claim: Ursula von der Leyen’s record as President of the European Commission shows a sustained and concrete commitment to human rights, expressed in her public positions and, more importantly, in binding EU legislation, enforcement actions inside the EU, and external human‑rights diplomacy.

Key evidence

- Public commitments and framing
  - In her 2020 State of the Union speech to the European Parliament, von der Leyen condemned “LGBTIQ‑free zones” as “humanity‑free zones” and pledged a “Union of Equality,” a rights‑first framing she has repeated since. This is recorded in Parliament’s verbatim transcript and on EU external action pages. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/doceo/document/CRE-9-2020-09-16_EN.html?utm_source=openai))
  - The Commission then launched and implemented an LGBTIQ Equality Strategy 2020–2025 and, under her second term, adopted its successor for 2026–2030. ([commission.europa.eu](https://commission.europa.eu/publications/lgbtiq-equality-strategy-2020-2025_en?utm_source=openai))

- Turning rights into binding EU law (proposals steered and dossiers delivered during her Commission)
  - AI Act: the world’s first comprehensive AI law, built around fundamental‑rights risk management (e.g., bans of unacceptable‑risk uses and duties for high‑risk systems). It entered into force in 2024. ([eur-lex.europa.eu](https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2024/1689/oj/eng))
  - Digital Services Act: protects users’ fundamental rights online (due process for takedowns, transparency, researcher access, protections for minors) and is actively enforced by the Commission. ([commission.europa.eu](https://commission.europa.eu/strategy-and-policy/priorities-2019-2024/europe-fit-digital-age/digital-services-act_en?utm_source=openai))
  - Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive (CSDDD): obliges large companies operating in the EU to identify, prevent, and remediate human‑rights harms across their chains of activity. Formally adopted in May 2024. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/24/corporate-sustainability-due-diligence-council-gives-its-final-approval/?utm_source=openai))
  - Forced‑labour ban regulation: the EU now prohibits products made with forced labour from the EU market, with investigative and enforcement powers for authorities and the Commission. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/11/19/products-made-with-forced-labour-council-adopts-ban/?utm_source=openai))

- Enforcing rights and the rule of law inside the EU
  - Her Commission activated the Rule‑of‑Law Conditionality mechanism for the first time, proposing measures to protect the EU budget where systemic rule‑of‑law breaches were found; the Council then suspended billions for Hungary based on the Commission’s assessment. This is a concrete use of EU tools to uphold rule‑of‑law and anti‑corruption standards. ([cna.org.cy](https://www.cna.org.cy/press-release/article/3850933/press-release-european-commission?utm_source=openai))
  - The EU acceded to the Istanbul Convention (entry into force for the EU on 1 October 2023), committing EU institutions and relevant policy areas to combat violence against women—another core human‑rights file advanced and concluded under her tenure. ([eur-lex.europa.eu](https://eur-lex.europa.eu/EN/legal-content/summary/eu-accession-to-the-istanbul-convention.html?utm_source=openai))

- External human‑rights policy and accountability
  - Under her Commission, the EU Global Human Rights Sanctions Regime has been maintained and expanded, targeting perpetrators of serious abuses worldwide. Listings have been renewed and enlarged through 2024–2025. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2023/12/04/eu-global-human-rights-sanctions-regime-restrictive-measures-prolonged/?utm_source=openai))
  - Von der Leyen personally backed accountability for Russia’s aggression and war crimes in Ukraine, including support for an international centre and a dedicated tribunal for the crime of aggression, and cooperation with the ICC. ([enlargement.ec.europa.eu](https://enlargement.ec.europa.eu/news/speech-president-von-der-leyen-accountability-summit-kyiv-2023-05-10_en?utm_source=openai))

- Managing migration with embedded safeguards
  - The 2024 Pact on Migration and Asylum is contested by NGOs, but the final instruments embed explicit safeguards (e.g., independent fundamental‑rights monitoring during screening and border procedures), with FRA mandated to guide and support implementation—showing an attempt to balance control with rights protections. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/14/the-council-adopts-the-eu-s-pact-on-migration-and-asylum/?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line: Across rhetoric, legislation, enforcement inside the Union, and external action, von der Leyen has repeatedly advanced and operationalised human‑rights protections—often in the form of concrete, enforceable EU rules and measures. While some policies (notably parts of the Migration Pact) face criticism from rights groups, the overall record supports the claim that she values human rights and has used the Commission’s agenda and tools to advance them. ([theguardian.com](https://www.theguardian.com/world/2024/apr/10/eu-passes-asylum-and-migration-pact-after-eight-years-of-deadlock?utm_source=openai))

Part 1 — Answer

Short version: You can’t directly “prove” a person’s inner values, but you can test them against sustained choices, laws delivered, money moved, and political capital spent. Since taking office in December 2019, Ursula von der Leyen has repeatedly advanced, defended, and enforced human-rights protections through EU law, funding decisions, and foreign-policy instruments. The clearest evidence:

- Put enforceable rights at the core of landmark EU tech laws
  - AI Act: bans rights‑threatening AI uses (e.g., biometric categorisation by sensitive traits; untargeted scraping for facial-recognition databases; emotion recognition at work/schools) and regulates high‑risk systems to safeguard fundamental rights. Agreed in 2023, adopted in 2024–2025 by Parliament and Council. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/news/en/press-room/20240308IPR19015/artificial-intelligence-act-meps-adopt-landmark-law?utm_source=openai))
  - Digital Services Act: the Commission’s own framework makes “protection of fundamental rights” a stated goal, giving users due‑process tools (explanations, appeals, data access) and imposing risk‑mitigation duties on very large platforms. Enforcement began 2023–2024. ([commission.europa.eu](https://commission.europa.eu/strategy-and-policy/priorities-2019-2024/europe-fit-digital-age/digital-services-act_en?utm_source=openai))

- Built and used an EU “Magnitsky‑style” global human‑rights sanctions regime
  - The EU created a worldwide framework (Dec 2020) to target perpetrators of genocide, torture, arbitrary detention, etc.; it has since been renewed and expanded under her Commission. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2020/12/07/eu-adopts-a-global-human-rights-sanctions-regime/?utm_source=openai))

- Advanced women’s rights and anti‑violence protections
  - EU accession to the Istanbul Convention (entered into force for the EU on Oct 1, 2023) binds EU institutions to robust standards against violence toward women; von der Leyen publicly framed it as a values‑based step (“Europe is on the side of women”). ([eur-lex.europa.eu](https://eur-lex.europa.eu/EN/legal-content/summary/eu-accession-to-the-istanbul-convention.html?utm_source=openai))

- Required companies to respect human rights in supply chains
  - Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive (CSDDD) adopted in 2024 obliges large companies to identify, prevent, and remedy human‑rights and environmental harms across their chains, with liability and penalties. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/24/corporate-sustainability-due-diligence-council-gives-its-final-approval/?utm_source=openai))

- Defended LGBTIQ people’s rights and the EU’s rule‑of‑law baseline
  - In her 2020 State of the Union address she condemned “LGBTIQ‑free zones” and pledged an EU “Union of Equality”; this was followed by an LGBTIQ Equality Strategy and concrete measures (e.g., denying certain EU grants to municipalities with discriminatory resolutions; Parliament later declared the EU an LGBTIQ Freedom Zone). ([enlargement.ec.europa.eu](https://enlargement.ec.europa.eu/news/state-union-address-president-von-der-leyen-european-parliament-plenary-2020-09-16_en?utm_source=openai))
  - Backed rule‑of‑law conditionality: the EU suspended €6.3bn of cohesion funds for Hungary in 2022 over rule‑of‑law breaches; the Commission has maintained those measures pending genuine fixes. Using the budget to defend core rights is costly and politically hard, yet sustained. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2022/12/12/rule-of-law-conditionality-mechanism/?utm_source=openai))

- Backed accountability for atrocity crimes
  - The EU co‑launched the Atrocity Crimes Advisory Group for Ukraine and has coordinated support for international accountability, aligning EU policy with human‑rights law in wartime. ([eeas.europa.eu](https://www.eeas.europa.eu/eeas/ukraine-european-union-united-states-and-united-kingdom-establish-atrocity-crimes-advisory-group-aca_en?utm_source=openai))

What about counter‑evidence? There are credible criticisms that elements of the EU’s migration policy during her tenure — notably the EU–Tunisia deal and follow‑on steps — conflict with human‑rights safeguards (non‑refoulement, treatment of migrants, and civil‑society space). Leading NGOs have argued the arrangements risk EU complicity in abuses; several MEPs and media have echoed oversight concerns. These are serious and fair critiques. But they show a tension in one policy area more than they negate the overall, multi‑year pattern above: passing and enforcing rights‑centred EU law; conditioning EU money on rights and rule‑of‑law standards; and deploying EU foreign‑policy tools to protect rights. On balance, the evidence from law, funding, and diplomacy indicates she does value human rights, while some migration files remain contested and merit stricter conditionality and monitoring. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/07/eu-tunisia-agreement-on-migration-makes-eu-complicit-in-abuses-against-asylum-seekers-refugees-and-migrants/?utm_source=openai))

1) Answer

Short version: The best evidence that Ursula von der Leyen values human rights is what she chose to propose, pass, and enforce as President of the European Commission. During her tenure (since December 1, 2019), the EU adopted and began enforcing multiple binding laws and policies that put fundamental rights at their core; she also backed accountability for grave abuses abroad. Highlights:

- Built fundamental-rights safeguards into landmark tech rules, then enforced them
  - Artificial Intelligence Act: first comprehensive AI law, expressly designed to protect fundamental rights (bans on social scoring, certain biometric uses, emotion recognition in schools/workplaces; risk-based duties for high‑risk AI). Proposed under her Commission and adopted in 2024. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/news/en/press-room/20240308IPR19015/artificial-intelligence-act-meps-adopt-landmark-law?utm_source=openai))
  - Digital Services Act: protects users’ fundamental rights online and creates powerful enforcement she used in practice (e.g., proceedings against Meta/TikTok for alleged DSA breaches). ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2022/10/04/dsa-council-gives-final-approval-to-the-protection-of-users-rights-online/?utm_source=openai))

- Advanced press freedom and the safety of journalists/human-rights defenders
  - Anti‑SLAPP Directive: EU‑wide procedural shields against abusive lawsuits targeting public participation, journalists, and human-rights defenders, adopted in 2024. ([commission.europa.eu](https://commission.europa.eu/strategy-and-policy/policies/justice-and-fundamental-rights/democracy-eu-citizenship-anti-corruption/democracy-and-electoral-rights/protecting-journalists-and-human-rights-defenders-strategic-lawsuits-against-public-participation_en?utm_source=openai))
  - European Media Freedom Act: new rules to protect media pluralism and journalistic independence. Announced by von der Leyen, adopted in 2024. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/03/26/european-media-freedom-act-council-adopts-new-rules-to-protect-journalists-and-media-providers/?utm_source=openai))

- Strengthened protection from gender‑based violence
  - First‑ever EU Directive to combat violence against women and domestic violence (criminalising FGM, forced marriage, and several online offenses; victim protection/support standards), adopted in 2024. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/07/council-adopts-first-ever-eu-law-combating-violence-against-women/?utm_source=openai))
  - EU accession to the Istanbul Convention (in force for the EU since October 1, 2023), committing EU institutions to robust standards on preventing and combating violence against women. ([eur-lex.europa.eu](https://eur-lex.europa.eu/EN/legal-content/summary/eu-accession-to-the-istanbul-convention.html?utm_source=openai))

- Promoted equality and non‑discrimination, including LGBTIQ+ rights
  - Publicly framed equality as a core EU value (e.g., State of the Union 2020: “LGBTIQ‑free zones are humanity‑free zones”) and launched the EU’s first LGBTIQ Equality Strategy 2020–2025, followed by its successor for 2026–2030. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/doceo/document/B-9-2021-0166_EN.html?utm_source=openai))
  - Started the legal process to add hate speech and hate crime to the Treaty’s list of EU crimes. ([commission.europa.eu](https://commission.europa.eu/strategy-and-policy/policies/justice-and-fundamental-rights/combatting-discrimination/racism-and-xenophobia/extending-eu-crimes-hate-speech-and-hate-crime_en?utm_source=openai))

- Used EU tools to respond to serious human‑rights abuses globally and to uphold rule of law at home
  - EU Global Human Rights Sanctions Regime (“EU Magnitsky”): adopted in 2020 and repeatedly prolonged; enables asset freezes and travel bans for grave abuses worldwide. While listings are formally by the Council, the regime’s creation and continued use align with her stated external‑action priorities. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2020/12/07/eu-adopts-a-global-human-rights-sanctions-regime/?utm_source=openai))
  - Rule‑of‑law conditionality on EU funds: under her Commission the mechanism was activated; billions were suspended for Hungary to protect the EU budget and Charter‑linked values. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2022/12/12/rule-of-law-conditionality-mechanism/?utm_source=openai))

- Put human rights into global supply chains and markets
  - Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive (CSDDD): adopted in 2024; requires large companies to identify, prevent and remedy adverse human‑rights and environmental impacts across their chains of activities. Proposed by her Commission. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/24/corporate-sustainability-due-diligence-council-gives-its-final-approval/?utm_source=openai))
  - Forced‑labour products ban: political deal in March 2024; regulation adopted by the Council in November 2024 to keep forced‑labour goods off the EU market. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/03/05/council-and-parliament-strike-a-deal-to-ban-products-made-with-forced-labour/?utm_source=openai))

- Backed accountability for atrocity crimes in Ukraine
  - Personally condemned atrocities (e.g., Bucha), supported evidence‑gathering, and championed the International Centre for the Prosecution of the Crime of Aggression, coordinating with the ICC and Eurojust. ([enlargement.ec.europa.eu](https://enlargement.ec.europa.eu/news/statement-president-ursula-von-der-leyen-following-her-phone-call-president-zelenskyy-commissions-2022-04-04_en?utm_source=openai))

What this does and does not prove: No leader’s entire record will satisfy every human‑rights group. For example, the 2024 Pact on Migration and Asylum—proposed by her Commission and adopted by the co‑legislators—has been criticised by Amnesty International for heightening risks of violations, even as EU institutions say it incorporates safeguards and solidarity. Still, the breadth of rights‑centred legislation initiated and delivered under her leadership, and concrete enforcement actions, are strong, public, and verifiable evidence that she values human rights and has used her office to advance them. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/04/eu-migration-asylum-pact-put-people-at-risk-human-rights-violations/?utm_source=openai))

**💭 OUTPUT MESSAGE:**


1) Answer to the prompt

The clearest way to “prove” a public leader values human rights is to show repeated, concrete decisions that prioritise protecting fundamental rights even when that requires new rules, enforcement, or international commitments. As European Commission President (Dec 2019–present), Ursula von der Leyen’s Commission initiated and helped deliver a series of human-rights–centred laws and measures:

- Forced-labour ban on products: She announced the initiative in her 2021 State of the Union (“Human rights are not for sale — at any price”) and the Commission tabled the proposal on 14 Sep 2022. The Parliament and Council reached a political deal in Mar 2024, and the Council formally adopted the regulation on 19 Nov 2024; it bars any product made with forced labour from the EU market and will apply after a transition period. ([ireland.representation.ec.europa.eu](https://ireland.representation.ec.europa.eu/news-and-events/news/2021-state-union-address-president-von-der-leyen-2021-09-15_en?utm_source=openai))

- Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive (CSDDD): Final Council approval on 24 May 2024 obliges large companies to identify, prevent, mitigate, and remedy adverse human-rights and environmental impacts in their own operations and value chains, with liability for harms. This is a direct, systemic human-rights due‑diligence requirement across supply chains. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/24/corporate-sustainability-due-diligence-council-gives-its-final-approval/?utm_source=openai))

- First EU law to combat violence against women and domestic violence: Proposed by the Commission in 2022 and adopted in 2024, it criminalises FGM, forced marriage, and several forms of online violence EU‑wide, and mandates victim protection and support. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/07/council-adopts-first-ever-eu-law-combating-violence-against-women/?utm_source=openai))

- Anti‑SLAPP directive protecting journalists and human-rights defenders: Adopted in Mar 2024 to curb abusive lawsuits that silence public‑interest speech. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/03/19/anti-slapp-final-green-light-for-eu-law-protecting-journalists-and-human-rights-defenders/?utm_source=openai))

- EU Artificial Intelligence Act + human‑rights–first AI treaty: The Council gave final green light to the AI Act in May 2024, a risk‑based regime that bans practices like social scoring and seeks to ensure respect for fundamental rights. In Sep 2024 the EU (via the Commission) also signed the Council of Europe’s Framework Convention on AI, the first global, legally binding AI treaty centred on human rights, democracy, and rule of law. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/21/artificial-intelligence-ai-act-council-gives-final-green-light-to-the-first-worldwide-rules-on-ai/?utm_source=openai))

- EU Global Human Rights Sanctions Regime (“EU Magnitsky”): Adopted in Dec 2020 under her Commission, giving the EU a standing tool to target perpetrators of serious human-rights violations worldwide; subsequently prolonged and used. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2020/12/07/eu-adopts-a-global-human-rights-sanctions-regime/?utm_source=openai))

- Rule‑of‑law conditionality enforcement: The Commission proposed measures in 2022 to protect the EU budget over rule‑of‑law breaches in Hungary; the Council suspended €6.3bn, and in 2023 the Commission confirmed measures should remain until deficiencies are remedied—tying EU funds to rule‑of‑law and fundamental‑rights safeguards. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2022/12/12/rule-of-law-conditionality-mechanism/?utm_source=openai))

- EU Anti‑Racism Action Plan (2020–2025) and “Union of Equality” agenda: Launched by the Commission with von der Leyen’s public commitment in 2020; the plan and its governance (including an EU anti‑racism coordinator) have guided work against structural discrimination. ([commission.europa.eu](https://commission.europa.eu/strategy-and-policy/policies/justice-and-fundamental-rights/combatting-discrimination/racism-and-xenophobia/eu-anti-racism-action-plan-2020-2025_en?utm_source=openai))

Taken together, these initiatives are strong, repeated evidence—across trade, corporate conduct, gender-based violence, media freedom, digital regulation, sanctions, rule‑of‑law enforcement, and anti‑discrimination—that von der Leyen values human rights and has used the tools of the EU to advance them.

Important nuance: Some policies pursued or supported by the Commission (e.g., elements of the EU Pact on Migration and Asylum and the 2023 EU‑Tunisia migration MoU) have drawn criticism from human‑rights organisations for risking violations at borders or in partner countries. That criticism does not negate the evidence above, but it shows her record is not without contested areas and trade‑offs. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/04/eu-migration-asylum-pact-put-people-at-risk-human-rights-violations/?utm_source=openai))


2) Sources I considered TRUSTWORTHY (with reasons)

- Source: Council of the European Union (Consilium press releases)
  Reasoning 1: It is an official EU institution publishing primary, authoritative records of legislative decisions by Member States; high editorial control.
  Reasoning 2: For this prompt, it provides definitive adoption dates and legal effects for the forced‑labour ban, AI Act, CSDDD, anti‑SLAPP law, violence‑against‑women directive, and human‑rights sanctions regime. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/11/19/products-made-with-forced-labour-council-adopts-ban/?utm_source=openai))

- Source: European Commission (official portals, DG pages, and representations)
  Reasoning 1: The Commission is the EU’s executive; its sites are the authoritative source for proposals, political priorities, and President/Commission statements.
  Reasoning 2: Directly relevant to von der Leyen’s human‑rights agenda: the Commission’s note linking the forced‑labour ban to her 2021 SOTEU; the Anti‑Racism Action Plan page with her commitment; and the violence‑against‑women directive explainer. ([single-market-economy.ec.europa.eu](https://single-market-economy.ec.europa.eu/news/commission-welcomes-political-agreement-ban-products-made-forced-labour-union-market-2024-03-05_en?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Council of Europe (treaty pages/press)
  Reasoning 1: Intergovernmental body and primary source for the AI Framework Convention; legally authoritative information on signatories and purpose.
  Reasoning 2: Confirms the EU’s signature of a binding AI treaty whose explicit aim is to align AI with human rights, democracy, and rule of law. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/portal/-/council-of-europe-opens-first-ever-global-treaty-on-ai-for-signature?utm_source=openai))

- Source: European External Action Service (EEAS — EU Delegation to the Council of Europe)
  Reasoning 1: Official EU diplomatic service; credible for formal acts taken by the EU in international fora.
  Reasoning 2: Confirms the Commission signed the AI Convention on the EU’s behalf and explains its human‑rights focus. ([eeas.europa.eu](https://www.eeas.europa.eu/delegations/council-europe/european-commission-signs-historic-council-europe-framework-convention-artificial-intelligence-and_en?s=51&utm_source=openai))

- Source: Reuters
  Reasoning 1: Global wire service with strong editorial standards and fact‑checking; widely used as a baseline by other outlets.
  Reasoning 2: Used sparingly to corroborate Parliament’s passage of key human‑rights legislation (e.g., violence‑against‑women directive) and milestones on the forced‑labour ban. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/eu-parliament-adopts-first-eu-wide-rules-combat-domestic-abuse-2024-04-24/?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Associated Press (AP)
  Reasoning 1: Independent, reputable wire service with rigorous editorial policies.
  Reasoning 2: Used for context on EU AI implementation tools (code of practice) and for migration‑policy developments when helpful; secondary to primary EU sources. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/a3df6a1a8789eea7fcd17bffc750e291?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Amnesty International
  Reasoning 1: Long‑standing, globally recognised human‑rights NGO with transparent methodologies; while advocacy‑oriented, it is a standard reference for rights impacts.
  Reasoning 2: Cited to fairly represent credible civil‑society criticism of parts of the EU migration agenda under von der Leyen, adding nuance to the overall assessment. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/04/eu-migration-asylum-pact-put-people-at-risk-human-rights-violations/?utm_source=openai))

**💭 OUTPUT MESSAGE:**


1) Answer

Short answer: Yes—on the evidence of her priorities, legislation proposed or steered by her Commission, enforcement choices, and repeated public positions, Ursula von der Leyen demonstrably treats the protection of human rights as a core value.

Key evidence

- Put human rights at the heart of EU external action. In 2020 the EU created the Global Human Rights Sanctions Regime (“EU Magnitsky”), which her Commission announced and prepared in line with her State of the Union pledge; it has since been maintained and expanded. This lets the EU target perpetrators of torture, slavery, arbitrary detention, etc., worldwide. ([eeas.europa.eu](https://www.eeas.europa.eu/eeas/sanctions-and-human-rights-towards-european-framework-address-human-rights-violations-and-abuses_und_en?utm_source=openai))

- Backed accountability for atrocity crimes. Von der Leyen has publicly supported the International Criminal Court and a dedicated tribunal for the crime of aggression against Ukraine; the EU set up an International Centre to prosecute that crime. Her Kyiv remarks were explicit: “Every victim deserves justice… Putin must be held accountable.” ([enlargement.ec.europa.eu](https://enlargement.ec.europa.eu/news/speech-president-von-der-leyen-accountability-summit-kyiv-2023-05-10_en?utm_source=openai))

- Enshrined fundamental-rights safeguards in tech policy. The landmark AI Act (proposed by her Commission, entered into force on 1 August 2024) bans rights‑abusive uses like social scoring and requires fundamental‑rights impact assessments for high‑risk AI deployments. The Council’s adoption note and Parliament’s summary both state the law’s purpose is to protect fundamental rights. ([commission.europa.eu](https://commission.europa.eu/news/ai-act-enters-force-2024-08-01_en?utm_source=openai))

- Protected refugees’ rights at scale. Following Russia’s 2022 invasion, the EU (with Commission leadership) activated and extended the Temporary Protection Directive, guaranteeing displaced Ukrainians residence, access to work, healthcare and schooling; in 2025 the Commission proposed extending protection to March 2027 and the Council agreed the extension trajectory. Von der Leyen explicitly framed this as protecting people fleeing war. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2022/03/04/ukraine-council-introduces-temporary-protection-for-persons-fleeing-the-war/?utm_source=openai))

- Fought forced labour. The Commission proposed, and the co‑legislators agreed, an EU‑wide ban on products made with forced labour; Parliament approved it in April 2024 and the Council gave final adoption in November 2024. The regulation creates investigations and market bans to keep forced‑labour goods out of the EU. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/eu-parliament-approves-ban-products-made-with-forced-labour-2024-04-23/?utm_source=openai))

- Advanced corporate human‑rights due diligence. The EU adopted the Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive (CSDDD) in May 2024, requiring large companies to identify, prevent and remedy human‑rights and environmental harms in their value chains, with civil liability for violations. This file originated from her Commission’s 2022 proposal. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2024/05/24/corporate-sustainability-due-diligence-council-gives-its-final-approval/?utm_source=openai))

- Used EU budget tools to defend rule of law (a human‑rights precondition) inside the Union. Under her Commission, the conditionality mechanism was applied to Hungary, suspending billions over rule‑of‑law risks; the Council relied on the Commission’s assessment. ([consilium.europa.eu](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/press/press-releases/2022/12/12/rule-of-law-conditionality-mechanism/?utm_source=openai))

- Took consistent equality positions. In her 2020 State of the Union address, she stated: “LGBTQI‑free zones are humanity‑free zones. They have no place in our Union,” and launched “Union of Equality” initiatives, including the Gender Equality Strategy and LGBTIQ Strategy. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/doceo/document/CRE-9-2020-09-16_EN.html?utm_source=openai))

- Framed migration management around values and rights. In July 2024 she told MEPs the new Pact on Migration and Asylum must be implemented “while fully respecting fundamental rights,” stressing migrants are “human beings… protected by human rights.” ([enlargement.ec.europa.eu](https://enlargement.ec.europa.eu/news/statement-european-parliament-plenary-president-ursula-von-der-leyen-candidate-second-mandate-2024-2024-07-18_en?utm_source=openai))

- Condemned unlawful attacks on civilians and backed humanitarian law. On 18 July 2024, she called for an “immediate and enduring ceasefire” in Gaza and highlighted humanitarian aid—an explicitly rights‑based position. ([enlargement.ec.europa.eu](https://enlargement.ec.europa.eu/news/statement-european-parliament-plenary-president-ursula-von-der-leyen-candidate-second-mandate-2024-2024-07-18_en?utm_source=openai))

Notes on limits and balance: Some measures backed by her Commission (e.g., the Migration and Asylum Pact) are criticized by NGOs as too restrictive; acknowledging debate does not negate the documented pattern above of prioritizing human‑rights tools, enforcement and rhetoric. ([theguardian.com](https://www.theguardian.com/world/2024/apr/10/eu-asylum-and-migration-pact-has-passed-despite-far-right-and-lefts-objections?utm_source=openai))

1) Answer

Short conclusion
Based on multiple, recent findings by independent human‑rights bodies, European institutions, courts and major newswires, there is strong, documentable evidence that the Meloni government (which Giorgia Meloni has led since October 2022) has adopted and defended policies that restrict or violate internationally protected rights, particularly on migration/asylum, freedom of assembly, media freedom, and LGBTQ+ family rights. The pattern across these files supports the claim that her government has shown disregard for key human‑rights obligations. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/italy?utm_source=openai))

Key evidence, by area

- Sea‑rescue obstruction and outsourcing migration control
  • Decree‑Law 1/2023 (the “Piantedosi” law, converted as Law 15/2023) restricts NGO rescues (one rescue per voyage; immediate sail to a distant port), with fines and detentions of ships and, since 2024–2025, even aircraft; UN experts and the Council of Europe warned these rules undermine the right to life at sea and breach Italy’s obligations. Italian authorities repeatedly detained NGO ships and grounded a rescue plane in 2024–2025 under these rules. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/the-italian-government-should-consider-withdrawing-decree-law-which-could-hamper-ngo-search-and-rescue-operations-at-sea?utm_source=openai))
  • Courts have repeatedly pushed back: in 2024 the Crotone civil court (confirmed on appeal in 2025) found Libya’s “coast guard” and MRCC are not legitimate SAR actors and struck down an NGO ship detention premised on obeying Libyan orders; in February 2024 Italy’s Court of Cassation upheld a conviction stemming from a 2018 “pushback” to Libya, reaffirming that returns to Libya are unlawful due to serious abuses there. Italy’s Constitutional Court heard a challenge to the rescue‑penalizing law on May 21, 2025. ([sos-humanity.org](https://sos-humanity.org/en/press/final-court-decision/?utm_source=openai))
  • HRW’s 2025 country chapter summarizes the government’s obstruction of rescues (25+ detentions of rescue ships through Sept 2024), the “distant port” policy, new powers to fine/detain NGO aircraft, and continued outsourcing to countries with poor records. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/italy?utm_source=openai))

- Offshoring asylum to Albania and unlawful detention
  • The November 2023 Italy‑Albania deal offshores processing and entails automatic detention. Amnesty warned in January 2024 this leads to arbitrary detention and undermines access to asylum and remedies; in October 2024 Rome’s immigration court refused to validate the first detentions in Albania and ordered people returned to Italy; in August 2025, the EU Court of Justice ruled on “safe country of origin” designations in a way that further undercut the legal basis used in the scheme. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/01/italy-mps-should-reject-unworkable-harmful-and-unlawful-migration-deal-with-albania/?utm_source=openai))

- Abusive conditions in migrant detention (CPR) centres
  • The Council of Europe’s anti‑torture watchdog (CPT) reported on Dec 13, 2024 that CPRs showed cases of ill‑treatment and excessive force by police, over‑medication with psychotropic drugs, carceral conditions and lack of independent monitoring—findings echoed by AP and the Financial Times. The CPT published Italy’s reply alongside its report. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/portal/-/anti-torture-committee-publishes-report-on-ad-hoc-visit-to-italy?utm_source=openai))
  • Amnesty (July 2024) found Italy’s migration detention system abusive, punitive and incompatible with international standards (inadequate legal process; prison‑like conditions; bans on phones; severe restrictions on movement). ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/07/italy-abuse-of-migration-related-detention-in-punitive-conditions-deprives-people-of-liberty-and-dignity/?utm_source=openai))

- Policing of protest and new “security” crackdown
  • Police baton‑charges against high‑school students at pro‑Palestinian protests in Pisa/Florence on Feb 23, 2024 drew condemnation from Italy’s President and sparked probes, showing excessive use of force against peaceful assembly. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/police-beatings-pro-palestinian-schoolchildren-spark-outrage-italy-2024-02-23/?utm_source=openai))
  • In spring–summer 2025, the government passed a security decree that criminalizes common protest tactics (e.g., traffic blockades), expands police powers, raises penalties in migrant centres, and funds legal defenses for officers under investigation. Amnesty called it “draconian” and incompatible with international law; Reuters reported Senate approval on June 4, 2025. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2025/05/italy-draconian-new-law-criminalizing-peaceful-protest-while-expanding-police-powers-must-be-rejected/?utm_source=openai))
  • Earlier (late 2022) the government had already introduced an “anti‑rave” law criticized by Article 19 for overbroad restrictions on assembly. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/2022/12/31/italy-parties?utm_source=openai))

- LGBTQ+ and family rights
  • In October 2024, Parliament made seeking surrogacy abroad a crime (up to 2 years’ prison and €1m fine). Reuters/AP reported, and HRW flagged the measure’s disproportionate impact on same‑sex and infertile couples’ ability to form a family. ILGA‑Europe’s 2025 Rainbow Map places Italy near the bottom of Europe on LGBTQI equality. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/italy-makes-it-illegal-seek-surrogacy-abroad-2024-10-16/?utm_source=openai))

- Media freedom and pressure on public broadcasting
  • RAI journalists went on strike (May 6, 2024) over “suffocating control” and alleged censorship; the European Commission’s 2024 Rule‑of‑Law report highlighted “undue political influence” risks at RAI; RSF’s 2025 Index shows Italy slipping to 49th globally, last in Western Europe. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/2024/05/06/italian-public-broadcaster-rais-journalists-strike-over-censorship-row?utm_source=openai))

- Anti‑discrimination concerns and policing
  • The Council of Europe’s ECRI flagged racial profiling by police (country assessment Oct 2024; public debate in May 2025); the government publicly dismissed the findings as “shameful,” rather than committing to corrective measures. The European Committee for Social Rights also found housing‑rights violations affecting Roma. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/italy-calls-council-europe-shameful-after-warning-police-racism-2025-05-29/?utm_source=openai))

Taken together—with repeated court setbacks, warnings from UN/CoE bodies, and legislative choices that tighten restrictions instead of addressing violations—the record demonstrates an ongoing disregard by Meloni’s government for several core human‑rights obligations (right to life at sea; liberty and due process; peaceful assembly; non‑discrimination; family life and equality). ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/italy?utm_source=openai))

1) Answer

Short answer: You can’t “prove” a person’s inner intent, but there is strong, recent, and well‑documented evidence from credible human-rights bodies and major newswires that several policies and actions pursued by Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni’s government have disregarded, subordinated, or chilled protected human rights (notably the rights to life and asylum at sea, liberty and due process in migration detention, freedom of assembly, non‑discrimination, reproductive autonomy, family life for LGBTQ+ families, and media freedom). Key evidence:

- At sea and externalisation of asylum
  - Decree Law 1/2023 and related practices (assigning distant ports; fines/impoundment of NGO ships) have been flagged by the Council of Europe’s Human Rights Commissioner as hampering life‑saving search-and-rescue and potentially breaching Italy’s human-rights and maritime-law obligations; Amnesty likewise warned these measures “will cost lives at sea.” Italy actually impounded NGO vessels under these rules (e.g., Humanity 1), with a court later finding one such detention unlawful. Together, these show a willingness to curtail humanitarian rescue despite foreseeable life‑and‑rights risks. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/the-italian-government-should-consider-withdrawing-decree-law-which-could-hamper-ngo-search-and-rescue-operations-at-sea?utm_source=openai))
  - The Italy–Albania offshoring scheme has repeatedly been halted by judges who refused to validate detentions and ordered people brought back to Italy, and has drawn detailed human‑rights objections from Amnesty. In 2025 the government expanded the scheme to use Albanian sites as repatriation hubs, and began transfers of rejected asylum seekers—again amid legal and rights concerns. This sustained push to externalise asylum processing, despite court setbacks and rights‑body warnings, evidences disregard for core protections. ([ansa.it](https://www.ansa.it/english/news/politics/2024/10/18/court-nixes-detention-of-migrants-taken-to-albania_3383d55c-fcae-46ad-b7fb-5a54d8073228.html?utm_source=openai))
  - Conditions in Italy’s migration detention (CPR) centres were harshly criticised by the Council of Europe’s anti‑torture committee (CPT): alleged ill‑treatment by police, overuse of psychotropic drugs, and lack of independent monitoring. Persisting with and expanding a system condemned by the CPT is incompatible with ensuring liberty, dignity and due process. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/0dba82e1f333c60b3bc49a0894970e9d?utm_source=openai))

- Policing and freedom of assembly/non‑discrimination
  - Police baton‑charges against largely student protesters (e.g., Pisa/Florence, Feb 2024) prompted national outcry and a presidential rebuke; interior officials nonetheless minimised them as “isolated.” In 2025, the Council of Europe’s anti‑racism body (ECRI) again urged Italy to address racial profiling; the government publicly attacked the finding. This pattern signals tolerance for excessive force and profiling concerns rather than remedial action. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/police-beatings-pro-palestinian-schoolchildren-spark-outrage-italy-2024-02-23/?utm_source=openai))

- Reproductive autonomy and women’s rights
  - Parliament authorized anti‑abortion groups’ access to public counselling clinics, a move medical groups and rights organizations warned would deter or pressure women seeking lawful abortion in a system already marred by high “conscientious objection” rates. Pushing this measure while barriers persist undermines effective access to a legal right. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/italy-passes-contested-plan-support-motherhood-abortion-clinics-2024-04-23/?utm_source=openai))

- LGBTQ+ family rights
  - The government advanced extraterritorial criminalisation of surrogacy and opposed EU measures for cross‑border recognition of same‑sex parentage; rights groups said these disproportionately harm LGBTQ+ families. Italy’s Constitutional Court later expanded protections (parental status and paternity leave for non‑biological mothers), but these judicial corrections do not erase the governing coalition’s restrictive policy drive. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/surrogate-parenthood-is-inhuman-italys-meloni-says-2024-04-12/?utm_source=openai))

- Media freedom and civic space
  - Independent monitors and the European press freedom index show deterioration: Italy fell to 49/180 in RSF’s 2025 index, amid SLAPPs, political lawsuits, and pressure on public broadcasting. The European Commission’s rule‑of‑law reporting and major outlets have highlighted increasing intimidation through legal actions and politicisation of media oversight. Persisting in such a climate, and sometimes fuelling it, chills free expression. ([rsf.org](https://rsf.org/en/country/italy?utm_source=openai))

- Cross‑cutting assessments
  - Human Rights Watch’s 2025 country report synthesises many of these concerns (migration externalisation to Albania, Tunisia cooperation, shrinking civic space, anti‑Roma rhetoric around criminal-law changes, and LGBTQ+ setbacks). Individually and cumulatively, these amount to a pattern of policy choices that knowingly place human-rights guarantees at risk. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/italy?utm_source=openai))

Conclusion: While Italy’s courts and independent institutions sometimes correct course, the government’s sustained pursuit and defence of measures repeatedly flagged by credible rights bodies and courts indicates disregard for the human‑rights impacts of its agenda.

2) Sources I considered trustworthy (with why)

- Human Rights Watch (HRW)
  - Reasoning 1: Long‑standing, methodologically transparent NGO with global field research and legal analysis; widely cited by courts, UN, and EU institutions.
  - Reasoning 2: Its 2025 Italy chapter aggregates primary developments (Albania deal rulings, Tunisia cooperation, civic‑space issues) with citations and legal framing directly relevant here. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/italy?utm_source=openai))

  - Reasoning 1: Premier global human‑rights NGO with rigorous documentation and clear methodological standards; frequently relied upon by international bodies.

1) Answer

There is substantial, multi‑source evidence from independent human‑rights institutions and courts that policies advanced by Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni’s government have curtailed or undermined protected rights—especially in migration/asylum, freedom of assembly, media freedom, and family/reproductive rights.

- Migration and asylum
  - NGO sea-rescue restrictions (Decree-Law 1/2023, then Law 15/2023) that fine/impound ships and force immediate sail to distant ports were flagged by the Council of Europe Commissioner for Human Rights as incompatible with states’ duties to safeguard life at sea and ensure effective rescue and disembarkation. EU-level documents and Italian courts have also questioned related detentions/fines. These measures, alongside impoundments, have been linked by charities and international bodies to heightened risks for people in distress. ([euronews.com](https://www.euronews.com/2023/02/03/italy-migrants-council-of-europe?utm_source=openai))
  - Externalizing asylum processing to Albania: The Italy–Albania deal has been criticized by the Council of Europe and Amnesty International for likely arbitrary/automatic detention, legal ambiguity, and weakened safeguards, risking violations of non‑refoulement and access to remedies. Despite political approval, early transfers were repeatedly blocked by magistrates, and the government has issued decrees to expand the centers’ remit. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/es/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/italy-albania-agreement-adds-to-worrying-european-trend-towards-externalising-asylum-procedures?utm_source=openai))
  - Use of “migration state of emergency,” accelerated border detention, age‑assessment measures, and continued pushbacks at Adriatic ports have drawn concern from the International Commission of Jurists and the Asylum Information Database (ECRE) for breaching EU/international law and exposing people to ill‑treatment or denial of asylum. ([icj.org](https://www.icj.org/italy-human-rights-should-be-protected-amidst-the-migration-state-of-emergency/?utm_source=openai))

- Freedom of assembly and policing
  - The 2022–2023 “anti‑rave”/public‑order changes were assessed by Article 19 as overbroad/vague and liable to chill peaceful assembly. In 2024–2025, baton charges against student demonstrations and a new security decree further tightening protest‑related offenses prompted national and international alarm. The President of the Republic publicly rebuked excessive force after schoolchildren were beaten in Pisa; later measures increased penalties for road blockades and granted added protections to law enforcement, which rights groups say restrain civic space. ([article19.org](https://www.article19.org/resources/italy-new-anti-rave-law-may-curtail-human-right-to-peaceful-assembly/?utm_source=openai))

- Media freedom and surveillance
  - Reporters Sans Frontières has warned that proposed and adopted “gag law” changes and broader trends risk limiting court‑reporting and investigative journalism; RSF also raised alarm over alleged spyware targeting a journalist, calling for government explanations. The Media Freedom Rapid Response mission and Italy’s journalists’ union (FNSI) likewise documented concerns about pressure on media and wiretap rules. ([rsf.org](https://rsf.org/fr/italie-rsf-appelle-les-s%C3%A9nateurs-%C3%A0-rejeter-une-proposition-de-loi-susceptible-d-emp%C3%AAcher-les?utm_source=openai))

- Anti‑racism and policing
  - The Council of Europe’s ECRI (2024 report; 2025 follow‑up) urged Italy to investigate racial profiling by police, especially affecting Roma and people of African descent, and to set up stronger accountability/equality mechanisms. Government leaders rejected the findings, but the recommendations stand as an official human‑rights assessment. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/portal/-/italy-should-set-up-an-independent-and-effective-equality-body-and-do-more-to-counter-hate-speech?utm_source=openai))

- Family and reproductive rights
  - In 2023 the Interior Ministry instructed municipalities to halt routine registration of both parents in some same‑sex families; the European Parliament condemned the suspension. In 2024 Parliament made surrogacy a “universal crime,” criminalizing Italians who pursue it abroad—measures widely criticized as discriminatory. In 2024 the Senate approved access for pro‑life groups in public counseling centers, which medical and rights groups warned could undermine effective abortion access amid already high conscientious‑objection rates. Italy’s Constitutional Court later restored recognition for two mothers on birth certificates—showing courts sometimes correct rights‑restrictive policies—but the government’s policy direction remains restrictive. ([ansa.it](https://www.ansa.it/english/news/politics/2023/03/30/ep-condemns-suspension-of-same-sex-adoption-registration_2e278362-9698-44b8-b817-b60d2b5cc70d.html?utm_source=openai))

- Overall pattern
  - Across migration control, public‑order, media, and equality policies, independent human‑rights bodies have repeatedly found risks of unlawful detention, impediments to life‑saving rescue, disproportionate limits on protest, pressure on the press, discriminatory policing, and curbs on family/reproductive rights. While the government disputes many critiques, the record from these institutions substantiates the claim that Meloni’s government has disregarded key human‑rights safeguards. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/italy-should-improve-legislation-and-practice-on-migration-and-asylum-women-s-rights-and-gender-equality?utm_source=openai))

1) Answer

Summary conclusion
There is substantial, well-documented evidence that a set of policies pursued by Giorgia Meloni’s government since October 2022 have conflicted with core human rights obligations (right to life at sea, liberty and due process, access to asylum and non‑refoulement, family life and non‑discrimination, and freedom of expression/press). This is shown by warnings from Europe’s principal human-rights bodies, repeated court defeats or referrals, and independent reporting on the consequences of those policies. Together, these amount to a pattern of disregard for human rights standards, even when formally justified as migration control or public order measures. Key evidence follows.

A) Hindering life‑saving sea rescues by NGOs
- In February 2023 the Council of Europe Commissioner for Human Rights formally urged Italy to withdraw or revise Decree‑Law 1/2023 (“NGO decree”) because provisions such as the “single-rescue” rule and assignment of distant ports “could hamper NGO search and rescue operations” and be at variance with Italy’s human‑rights and maritime obligations. Italy continued applying these rules. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/commissioner/news/2023/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/the-italian-government-should-consider-withdrawing-decree-law-which-could-hamper-ngo-search-and-rescue-operations-at-sea?utm_source=openai))
- Under this decree Italy repeatedly impounded NGO rescue ships and imposed fines after multi‑rescue operations, despite distress alerts and threats from armed Libyan units—conduct the NGOs say put lives at risk. These impoundments are documented by AP, Reuters and MSF. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/0039d1268bdafb6f6a2c78c7eb0414c9?utm_source=openai))

B) Expanding detention and curtailing access to asylum (“Cutro” package)
- After the 26 February 2023 Cutro shipwreck, the government curtailed “special protection,” introduced accelerated border procedures tied to “safe country” lists and raised immigration‑detention up to 18 months—measures criticized as incompatible with EU reception standards and proportionality. ([thenews.com.pk](https://www.thenews.com.pk/print/1111275-italy-extends-detention-period-to-curb-migrant-crossings?utm_source=openai))
- Italian courts repeatedly struck down or questioned these measures: the Catania court ruled detaining asylum seekers from “safe” countries during the accelerated border procedure unlawful under EU law; Italy’s Supreme Court referred related questions to the EU Court of Justice; and judges refused to validate detentions tied to a “bail” payment. The government publicly criticized those rulings rather than pausing the measures. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/doceo/document/E-9-2023-003065_EN.html?utm_source=openai))

C) Outsourcing asylum and detention to Albania
- The November 6, 2023 Italy‑Albania protocol created Italian‑run facilities in Albania to detain people intercepted at sea and process asylum claims. Amnesty International’s legal analysis warned it is “harmful and unlawful,” enabling automatic, prolonged detention with serious obstacles to effective remedies—i.e., a high risk of arbitrary detention and refoulement. Parliament nevertheless ratified the deal in 2024 and the centers opened in October 2024. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2024/01/italy-mps-should-reject-unworkable-harmful-and-unlawful-migration-deal-with-albania/?utm_source=openai))
- Implementation faltered amid legal challenges, minimal transfers, and subsequent repurposing to hold rejected asylum seekers pending removal. In 2025, the EU’s top court rebuked aspects linked to “safe country” designations in the Albania transfers, and Italy instead began sending people with rejected claims to Albania for detention—confirming rights‑risk concerns. ([dw.com](https://www.dw.com/en/has-italys-albania-migrant-deal-completely-failed/a-71694722?utm_source=openai))

D) Persisting cooperation enabling pullbacks to abuse in Libya
- The UN Independent Fact‑Finding Mission concluded in March 2023 that crimes against humanity have been committed against migrants in Libya’s detention system, and warned that EU and member‑state support to Libyan interception operations facilitates violations of non‑refoulement. Despite this, Italy maintained and prepared to renew its Libya cooperation framework; HRW in October 2025 called for its cancellation due to the predictable harm it enables. Italy also sanctioned NGO ships for not following Libyan Coast Guard instructions, even when those units acted abusively. Together this shows persistence with a policy known to result in serious abuses. ([unognewsroom.org](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/1545/fact-finding-mission-libya-press-conference-27-march-2023?utm_source=openai))

E) Family life and non‑discrimination: same‑sex parents
- In 2023, the Interior Ministry instructed municipalities to stop registering both same‑sex parents on children’s birth certificates, triggering prosecutions and deregistrations in cities like Padua. In May and July 2025 Italy’s Constitutional Court had to step in to restore recognition of both mothers at birth and equal leave—indicating the government’s stance had undermined children’s and families’ rights until overruled. ([lemonde.fr](https://www.lemonde.fr/en/intimacy/article/2024/10/18/in-italy-lesbian-couples-fight-to-retain-their-parental-status_6729710_310.html?utm_source=openai))

F) Freedom of expression and press
- Italy’s press‑freedom ranking fell in 2025; RSF and Italian journalist unions warned about a “gag law” approach to publishing judicial materials, the persistence of criminal defamation, and chilling lawsuits by powerful officials. Independent reporting also revealed government‑approved spyware surveillance targeting a migrant‑rescue NGO leadership. These show a climate detrimental to media freedom and civic space. ([ansa.it](https://www.ansa.it/amp/english/news/2025/05/02/italy-slips-to-49th-place-for-press-freedom-rsf_aa00f1f2-f423-46eb-8113-27d47a3754de.html?utm_source=openai))

Taken together: repeated warnings from human‑rights institutions, adverse rulings or referrals by courts, and credible reporting on consequences demonstrate that Meloni’s government has repeatedly advanced, defended, and persisted with measures that curtail rights—rather than adjusting course when risks were flagged—amounting to a pattern of disregard for human rights obligations.
